Law and Order for Typestate with Borrowing
Hannes Saffrich, Yuki Nishida, Peter Thiemann

Abstract
. Typestate systems are notoriously complex as they require sophisticated machinery for tracking aliasing. We propose a new, transition-oriented foundation for typestate in the setting of impure functional programming. Our approach relies on ordered types for simple alias tracking and its formalization draws on work on bunched implications. Yet, we support a flexible notion of borrowing in the presence of typestate. Our core calculus comes with a notion of resource types indexed by an ordered partial monoid that models abstract state transitions. We prove syntactic type soundness with respect to a resource-instrumented semantics. We give an algorithmic version of our type system and prove its soundness. Algorithmic typing facilitates a simple surface language that does not expose tedious details of ordered types. We implemented a typechecker for the surface language along with an interpreter for the core language.

1.
Introduction

Typestate
(
Strom and Yemini, 1986
;
Strom and Yellin, 1993
)
is a programming concept that incorporates information about the state of an
object into its type. In traditional type systems, types describe the structure of
objects, but they do not directly capture behavioral information
about the possible states an object can be in during its
lifetime. Typestate, on the other hand, encodes the current state of
an object in its type and the state transitions in the types of functions and methods.

Typestate systems contribute to writing safe, understandable, and
maintainable code, particularly in systems with complex behavior
of objects. The code is safe because the
type checker prevents accessing an object in an invalid state or
performing operations in the wrong order. The code is understandable
because types make the objects’ state explicit. Taken
together, typestate systems improve maintainability
because they eliminate out-of-band uses of objects at compile
time.

Early typestate systems require full ownership of all objects under
the typestate regime. Here, ownership means that at every point of
time there is a single reference to the object and all operations on
the object are conveyed via that reference. In particular, these
systems disallow aliases and passing the object to a function
transfers its ownership from caller to callee.

Borrowing refers to the act of temporarily loaning a reference to a
resource (e.g., an object) without
transferring ownership. The concept currently receives a lot of attention due
to its widespread use in the Rust language

(
Matsakis and Klock II, 2014
;
Team, 2024
)
: When
you borrow a reference to a resource in
Rust, the borrower (typically a function or a block of code) can read
or mutate the resource, but it does not take ownership of it. In
this context ownership means the responsibility for any cleanup
actions and deallocation of the resource.

The combination of typestate and borrowing is challenging, as
mutable borrows can change the typestate of the
borrowed object.
Problems like this have been addressed in various ways and are related
to constraining and reasoning about
aliasing.
Clarke et al. (2013)
presents an overview of several
approaches, but they can be very complex. For example, fractional
permissions
(
Boyland, 2003
)
provide a
fine-grained way to split an owning reference into several
aliases with different permissions and combine them later to regain full ownership.

In this paper, we propose a new angle of attack that is inspired by
non-commutative logic and ordered type systems. Non-commutative logic
is a substructural logic where neither weakening, nor contraction, nor
exchange of assumptions is allowed. Thus, it is a kind of linear
logic where assumptions (i.e., resources) must be consumed in the order
in which they were introduced. Such systems have been investigated
previously in the context of linguistics and logic, starting with the Lambek
calculus
(
Lambek, 1958
;
Lambek, 1960
)
and further studied by

Abrusci (1990)
. In a programming context,
Polakow, Pfenning, and DeYoung
studied ordered logic, mainly exploring
the proof theory
(
Polakow and Pfenning, 1999a
;
Polakow and Pfenning, 1999b
)
, applications to linear logic programming

(
Polakow, 2000
)
, to exceptions

(
Polakow and Yi, 2001
)
, and applications to concurrency theory

(
DeYoung, 2020
)
.
None of these works study the impact of ordering in an effectful call-by-value
language as we do.

OF
CF
r,w
c

Figure 1
.
Simple life cycle of file handles; OF=
(r|w)^{*}c
(open file);
CF=
\varepsilon
(closed file)

For illustration, let us consider the well-known example
of file handles (cf.
Figure

1
). Opening a
file yields a file handle that is ready for read, write, or close
operations. After a read or write operation, we can read, write, or
close again, but no further operations are
possible after a close.

Our system can model this example with regular languages for
the different states. In particular, the typestate of a file handle is
the language of all admissible traces of operations, that is, a
language on the symbols
r
,
w
, and
c
. We use
regular expressions to denote the languages and we assume that read
and write operations work on single bytes.

⬇

[

(

|

)
∗
c

]

:

[

\multimap

Byte

:

[

]

\otimes

Byte

\multimap

()

:

Handle
[

c

]

\multimap

()

This example indicates ownership with a language ending in

c
; any other handles indexed with languages not ending in

c
are borrowed. That is, the
read
and
write

operations actually work on borrows; only
close
requires
ownership. A function that first reads and then writes to a borrowed
handle takes an argument of type
Handle
[

rw

]
.
Handles are generally linear in our. A handle of type
Handle
[

\varepsilon

]
(for the
empty trace) is exhausted: no further operations are possible and the
reference can be discarded.

We express borrowing with a
split
primitive that consumes
a resource
h
and returns two (aliased)
references
bh
(“borrow of
h
”) and
h
of
the same underlying resource. These references may
have different typestates and we have to make sure that
the borrow
bh
is processed to the end before resuming
operations on
h
. At this point, the
ordered aspect of our
type system
comes into play:

⬇

/
m_{2}

:

Handle
[

m_{0}

]

\multimap

(
Handle
[

m_{1}

]

\odot

Handle
[

m_{2}

])

The
split
consumes a handle in state
m_{0}
and returns an
ordered
pair
of handles in states
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, respectively. This split is subject to
the correctness condition that
m_{1}\cdot m_{2}\subseteq m_{0}
. This
condition imposes that any trace of operations on the

Handle
[

m_{1}

]
object followed by any trace of
operations on the
Handle
[

m_{2}

]
object yields a trace on

Handle
[

m_{0}

]
. The elimination rule of the
ordered pair makes sure that the
m_{1}
handle is processed first
before operations on the
m_{2}
handle can start.

Here are some viable split instances (just the types without the
annotation) for the example. The result consists of the
new borrow (left) and the remaining resource (right).

Handle

right to do anything and then close.

Read-only re-borrow: the incoming handle is already borrowed as
there is no right for closing. We split into a re-borrow that is read-only (
r*
) and return a handle in the original read-write state.

(

]

This split is needed to apply the
close
operation: we split a
file handle with all capabilities into one owning reference to close
and a dangling reference that can be discarded.

⬇

split

:

[

(
r
|

)
∗

]

\multimap

Handle
[

r
∗
c

]

\odot

Handle
[

\varepsilon

]

The final example demonstrates that the split can be used for more
than just borrowing. This split implements a safe cast from an owning
read-write handle to an owning read-only handle.

The actual calculus that we present handles states more abstractly. We
only demand that states are drawn from an
ordered partial
monoid
(OPM) (see
Section

2.1
). In our example, the set of regular languages

\{L\mid L\subseteq(r|w)^{*}c^{?}\}
is an OPM under language
concatenation and ordered by language
inclusion, as we will show in
Example

2.2
.

As we remarked above, there is plenty of work on typestate as well as
on controlling aliasing. In the context of Java,

Bierhoff and Aldrich (2007)
discuss a closely related typestate system
that supports aliasing. Their system is
fundamentally different from ours. It is
state-based
as it specifies a
transition as a pair of input and output state, whereas our
system is
transition-based
as it specifies a transition by an element of an OPM, that is, an abstraction
of a set of traces of operations performed by the method.
Moreover, their system supports refined state transitions that depend on the
run-time result of a method call. Thus, each method is annotated with a
state-change formula. Access to a resource is governed
by a permission which controls the right to write and
change the state. Their notion of splitting applies to
the permission when creating an alias. Our system does not
have built-in support for controlling read/write permissions (like
immutable borrows), but we can model that notion with a suitably
chosen OPM (cf. the read-only borrow in the above file example).

The calculus in
Garcia et al. (2014)
is very close
to the one by

Bierhoff and Aldrich (2007)
, but simplifies the handling of
states, presents further refinements to fine-tune the handling of
typestate, and proposes gradual typing to include run-time checks for
typestates. Their model can control interleaved operations on aliases
to some extent; our system allows for multiple aliases, but
requires that they are processed in order.

Contributions

(1)

We identify the notion of an ordered partial monoid as a minimal
specification for typestate with borrowing (
Section

2
).

(2)

We define a typed lambda calculus that supports
intuitionistic as well as linear and ordered constructions (
Section

3
);
type constructions like function space and product come
in several variants that differ in their ordering properties; the
calculus requires a tree-like typing environment as in the
logic of bunched implications

(
O’Hearn and Pym, 1999
)
.

(3)

We establish the syntactic metatheory for type soundness, which
incorporates protocol adherence for
objects with typestate and borrowing (
Section

3.4
).

(4)

We present a syntax-driven version of the type system
(
Section

4
) as a basis for our prototype
implementation of the type checker. Type checking is decidable if
certain operations on the underlying OPM are decidable.

2.
Motivation

In this section, we formally introduce OPMs and illustrate them with
examples. Then we move on to consider splitting and investigate some
ramifications of our type system with ordered resources. We illustrate
the issues by dissecting two representative constructs: ordered
product elimination and let expressions.

The full system in
Section

3
supports three kinds of types:
unrestricted types, linear types,
and orderer types for resources. This distinction governs the way that
type assumptions of variables are treated in typing contexts:
variables of unrestricted type are subject to exchange, contraction,
and weakening; linear variables are subject to exchange; ordered
variables are subject to nothing.

For simplicity, we focus on linear and
ordered types in this section. Unrestricted types, such as

Int
and
Bool
, introduce additional
complexity that we want to avoid in this initial discussion.

2.1.
Ordered Partial Monoids

In the introduction, we already claimed that ordered partial monoids
are an appropriate abstraction for resource management with
borrowing. Let us first define this notion.

Definition 2.1.

A
partial monoid
is a structure
(M,\odot,\varepsilon)
such that

•

multiplication
\odot:M\times M\hookrightarrow M
is a partial function;

•

there is a neutral element
\varepsilon\in M
with respect to

\odot
, that is,
\forall m\in M
,

m\odot\varepsilon=\varepsilon\odot m=m
where
multiplication with the neutral element is always defined;

•

multiplication is associative:
\forall m,n,o\in M
,
(m\odot n)\odot o=m\odot(n\odot o)
where the left side is
defined if and only if the right side is defined.

A partial monoid is
ordered
(OPM), if there is a preorder
{\leq}\subseteq M\times M
such that multiplication is monotone and
downwards closed:

\forall m,m^{\prime},n,n^{\prime}
, if
m\leq m^{\prime}
and
n\leq n^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}
is defined, then
m\odot n
is defined and

m\odot n\leq m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}
.
∎

This definition is not without precedent.
Partially-ordered
commutative
monoids are used for
constructing Kripke models for BI

(
O’Hearn and Pym, 1999
)
.
Galmiche et al. (2005)

even makes commutativity optional. Both derive their ordering from the
multiplication, rather than requiring a separate order relation.

The basic idea is that we regard the elements of the OPM as
abstractions of admissible sequences of operations on a
resource. Thus, a resource type is indexed with some OPM element
m
,
an operation on a resource expects a resource with a type index that
abstracts the singleton sequence of just this operation, taken
together we expect that the indexes of all operations on the resource must
multiply up to some
m^{\prime}
with
m^{\prime}\leq m
.

Once a resource has index
m
that contains
\varepsilon
(i.e.,

\varepsilon\leq m
), we
consider it
droppable
; a resource with index
\varepsilon
is

exhausted
in the sense that no further operation is applicable. We choose to keep resources
linear so that our calculus provides an explicit
\mathtt{drop}
operation
to discard exhausted resources. We omit
\mathtt{drop}
in our
examples, assuming that the typechecker inserts it
automatically.
1
1

1

Alternatively, the type system could change
the substructural properties of a resource with index
\varepsilon

to affine, so that it could be dropped by weakening.

Example 2.2.

The OPM for file permissions in the introduction is given by the
set of regular languages, which are subsets of a prefix language of
the
envelope language

E=(r|w)^{*}c
. That is,
M=\{R\mid R\subseteq(r|w)^{*}c^{?},R\text{ regular}\}
.

•

Multiplication is the usual language concatenation, with the
restriction that the result must be in
M
. For example, the product

rc\odot(r|w)^{*}
is not defined in
M
, because
rc(r|w)^{*}

contains the word
cw
which is not a prefix of an element of
E
.

•

The neutral element is
\{\varepsilon\}
, the language of the
empty word.

•

Multiplication is associative and the definedness condition is
fulfilled.

•

The ordering is language inclusion; monotonicity of
multiplication is a standard result in formal language theory;
downwards closedness is easy to see.

Technically, the languages involved need not be regular. We phrase
the example in terms of regular languages because it makes all
operations and relations (and hence type checking) decidable.

\begin{array}[]{r|ccc}\odot&\varepsilon&b&\ast\\
\hline\cr\varepsilon&\varepsilon&b&\ast\\
b&b&b&\ast\\
\ast&\ast&-&-\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{r|ccc}\leq&\varepsilon&b&\ast\\
\hline\cr\varepsilon&\bullet&\bullet&-\\
b&-&\bullet&-\\
\ast&-&-&\bullet\\
\end{array}

(a)

OPM for ownership and mutable borrows

\multimap

[

]

\multimap

\multimap

OPM and API for ownership and mutable borrows

Example 2.3.

Here is an OPM for ownership and mutable borrows.
Let
M=\{\varepsilon,b,\ast\}
(discardable, borrowed, owned
reference) with multiplication and ordering defined by the tables in

Figure

2(a)
. The intuition of multiplication
derives from splitting: if the left multiplication with
\ast
were
defined for any element but
\varepsilon
, a split like
\ast\odot x
for
x\neq\varepsilon
would be valid. But that would mean, we
could first destroy the resource (exerting full ownership), but afterwards
we would still hold a valid handle at state
x
, which is impossible.
Right multiplication with
\ast
or
b
controls borrowing:
We can split borrows from owning references as
b\odot\ast\leq\ast
. We
can also split borrows from borrows as
b\odot b\leq b
.
An API for references would look similar to the file API (see

Figure

2(b)
).

Example 2.4.

Session types
(
Hüttel et al., 2016
)
is a
generic term for behavioral type systems that
guarantee communication properties like type soundness, protocol fidelity, and
deadlock freedom for concurrent systems. In a nutshell, a session
type is ascribed to a communication channel and it describes the
protocol on this channel, i.e., the possible sequences of
interaction on this channel.

Context-free session types (CFST) have been proposed as an extension of
traditional session types embedded in a functional language, but
with fewer restrictions on recursion

(
Almeida et al., 2022
)
. The original system has
been extended in several ways including subtyping

(
Silva et al., 2023
)
. The latter publication describes
the syntax for session types like this:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle::=\mathtt{skip}\mid S;S\mid{!T}\mid{?T}\mid\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}\mid\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}\mid\mu X.S\mid X

open

\displaystyle S^{\bot}

\displaystyle::=\mathtt{end!}\mid\mathtt{end?}\mid S;S^{\bot}\mid\oplus\{l_{i}:S^{\bot}_{i}\}\mid\&\{l_{i}:S^{\bot}_{i}\}\mid S^{\bot};\mathtt{skip}

terminated

Briefly spoken, a communication channel is created with a terminated type of
the form
S^{\bot}
. The type
skip
indicates a noop; the type

S_{1};S_{2}
stands for the sequential composition of the protocols

S_{1}
and
S_{2}
;
{!T}
and
{?T}
are protocols that send or
receive one item of type
T
, the type of messages which we leave
unspecified here; the selection type
\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}
lets the
sender choose a label
l_{j}
and continue with the corresponding
protocol
S_{j}
; dually, the branch type
\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}
receives
a label
l_{j}
and continues with the chosen
S_{j}
; the type
\mu X.S
is an equirecursive session type with type variable
X
; and

X
refers to a variable bound by a recursive type.
The nonterminal
S^{\bot}
characterizes the possible endings of a
session type. The types
end!
and
end?
stand for
two different ways of closing a channel (active and passive); the
remaining cases are just special cases of
S
tailored to the
situation at the end. Importantly, all continuations
have to be uniform in selection and branching, either open or terminating.

This syntax is subject to laws that make CFSTs a partial monoid with
neutral element
skip
and sequential composition as
multiplication. (Equi-) Recursive types come with the usual guardedness
restriction that disallows empty types like
\mu X.X
. Moreover,
composition distributes from the right
over selection
\oplus
and branching
\&
.

A suitable partial order for this OPM is the subtyping relation proposed by

Silva et al. (2023)
. Sequential composition is
monotone with respect to subtyping as this requirement is an
inference rule in the definition of subtyping.
Another suitable partial order is, of course, the equivalence
relation on types defined by the monoid laws.

The literature
(
Almeida et al., 2022
)
advocates an
interface based on channel passing, which is concisely described using
polymorphism on the continuation session
\alpha
:

⬇

send

:

\forall

\alpha
.

T

\multimap

Chan
[

!
T
;
\alpha

]

\multimap

Chan
[

\alpha

]

recv

:

\forall

\alpha
.

Chan
[

?
T
;
\alpha

]

\multimap

(
T

\otimes

Chan
[

\alpha

])

The functions
send
and

recv
both consume a channel of suitable (linear) type

Chan
[

S

]
, they modify it according to the operation,
and return the channel in its updated state and type. In this
system, all session types have the form
S^{\bot}
as channel references are linear, they
imply ownership, and consequently they must be closed in the end.

Combining CFST with our approach to borrowing, we find good use for types of
the form
S
: they correspond to the type of
borrowed channels
whereas
S^{\bot}

characterizes an owning reference of a channel.
The resulting concise API uses borrows
instead of polymorphism on sessions.

⬇

send_borrow

:

T

\multimap

Chan
[

!
T

]

\multimap

()

recv_borrow

:

Chan
[

?
T

]

\multimap

T

Comparing the types, we see that the channel-passing operations peel
off the first operation from the type of the owning reference. In
contrast, the API using borrowing does not require ownership and it
is not concerned with managing the continuation: it just gets the
right to send or receive a single
T
on the channel.

2.2.
Splitting and Order

The key operation to create a borrow is the
split
shown in
the introduction:
2
2

2

We drop the prefix
Handle
as
the discussion is generic for all resources.

⬇

split@m1
/
m_{2}

:

[

m_{0}

]

\multimap

([

m_{1}

]

\odot

[

m_{2}

])

Its indices are values from the OPM:
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
. The first

m_{1}
describes the traces that we want to grant to the borrow, the
second
m_{2}
describes the remaining traces that are admissible after
we have exercised
m_{1}
on the borrow. The soundness condition is
m_{1}\odot m_{2}\leq m_{0}
, which states that every composition of traces from

m_{1}
and
m_{2}
is still a valid trace on the original resource
described by
m_{0}
.

As a linear function,
split
consumes the argument of type

{[m_{0}]}
and returns an
ordered pair
of type

[m_{1}]\odot[m_{2}]
. An ordered pair comes with the
obligation to
process all resources in the first component before
starting to process any resource in the second component
.

An elimination rule for ordered pairs has to reflect this ordering in
the typing context
\Gamma
.
We express ordering contraints by having two distinct context formers.
This idea is inspired by the logic of bunched implications (BI)

(
O’Hearn and Pym, 1999
)
, where one context former allows
for weakening and contraction; but the other does not.
Our context formers have a different interpretation: neither allows
for weakening and contraction; one allows for exchange, and the other
does not.

\displaystyle\Gamma\Coloneqq\kern 5.0pt

\displaystyle\cdot

empty context

\displaystyle x\mathord{:}T

singleton binding;

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

no exchange: bindings from
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}

must be processed before bindings from
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
;

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

exchange: bindings from
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
are
independent from those in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
; no order imposed.

As in BI, the context formers can be arbitrarily nested.
Both context formers are subject to the monoid laws with unit
\cdot
and
parallel composition is commutative.
We also define
context patterns

\mathcal{G}
as contexts with a hole
that can be filled with a context
\Gamma
.

To illustrate the definition, we discuss some typing rules of our
system in simplified form, sound but more restrictive than necessary. Recall the rules for
linear pairs
. In a
linear context, all binders are mutually independent so contexts are
built solely with
\|
.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{1}\vdash M:T_{1}\otimes T_{2}\\
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathtt{let}~(x_{1}\otimes x_{2})=M~\mathtt{in}~N:T\end{array}}

(
\otimes
-E
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\quad\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M\otimes N:S\otimes T\end{array}}

(
\otimes
-I
)

For linear types, the notation
\Gamma_{1}\|\Gamma_{2}
splits the
incoming typing context
\Gamma
into contexts with disjoint domains,
that is,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. The
resources in
\Gamma_{1}
are consumed by evaluating
M
. After
elimination the new resources obtained by splitting are added to the
remaining resources in
\Gamma_{2}
. The introduction rule is also standard.

For a left-to-right ordered product, we have to keep in mind that the
system is intended for left-to-right call-by-value evaluation.
We have to split the incoming context
sequentially, indicated by the operator “,”. The reading of the assumption
\Gamma_{1}\vdash M:T_{1}\odot T_{2}
is that the resources in
\Gamma_{1}
have been
converted to the resource
T_{1}\odot T_{2}
, but they have not
necessarily been fully processed. To ensure that they are processed
before
\Gamma_{2}
, we have to put them in front of the context.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:T_{1}\odot T_{2}\quad\quad x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathtt{let}~(x_{1}\odot x_{2})=M~\mathtt{in}~N:T\end{array}}

(
\odot
-E1
)

This rule is sound, but restrictive in two respects. First, we cannot
assume that the incoming context
\Gamma
has the sequential form
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
expected by the rule. It is always possible to weaken the context
\Gamma
to
match the expectation by imposing extra (unnecessary) sequencing constraints.
Second, the rule sequentializes uses of
x_{1}

and
x_{2}
with uses of potentially unrelated resources in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. If
two resources are independent (e.g., two different
file handles or two distinct communication channels), then they
should not unduly constrain one another.

Hence, we restrict this elimination rule to
terms
M
that do not use any operations on resources.
To this end, we extend typing with a simple effect system that only
checks the existence of resource operations: the effect
0

characterizes a pure term, whereas effect
1
indicates that an
operation may be performed.
With this extra information, the elimination rule can be much more
liberal: It is sufficient if we can find the resources
\Gamma
needed
for
M
somewhere in the context as prescribed by context pattern

\mathcal{G}
. Then we can replace those resources by the components of the pair.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash M:T_{1}\odot T_{2}\mid 0\quad\quad\mathcal{G}[x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\vdash N:T\mid e\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{G}[\Gamma]\vdash\mathtt{let}~(x_{1}\odot x_{2})=M~\mathtt{in}~N:T\mid e\end{array}}

(
\odot
-E2
)

To make this rule generally applicable, we have to move resource operations
out of the elimination as done in A-normal form
(
Flanagan et al., 1993
)
. That is,
we transform
\mathtt{let}~(x_{1}\odot x_{2})=M~\mathtt{in}~N
to

\mathtt{let}~w=M~\mathtt{in}~\mathtt{let}~(x_{1}\odot x_{2})=w~\mathtt{in}~N
. This transformation, called let-insertion, shifts
our concerns about resource operations
to the typing of the normal
let
expression, where we can
solve them once and for all.

Before we discuss the
let
expression, it remains to discuss
the introduction rule for the ordered product. Here, the easiest
option is to require that the components are effect-free. A
preprocessing step that performs let-insertion for effect-ful
components guarantees general applicability.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid 0\quad\quad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\mid 0\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M\odot N:S\odot T\mid 0\end{array}}

(
\odot
-I
)

Several typing rules are possible for
let
.
Here are the two extreme cases.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\\
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel x\mathord{:}S\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathtt{let}\,x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}

(
let-
\|
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\\
x\mathord{:}S,\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\mathtt{let}\,x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}

(
let-
,
)

Rule (
let-
\|
) requires disjointness of the resources of

M
and
N
. Hence, the resources in
x
are also disjoint to those of

N
.
Rule (
let-
,
) forces a sequential split of the incoming
resources and puts the processing of
x
in front of any other
resource operation in
N
.

While these rules are sound, rule (
let-
\|
) is only
applicable if resources are disjoint. Rule (
let-
,
) is
always applicable, but it may enforce more sequentiality than
necessary. To see that, consider
\mathtt{let}~z=x_{1}~\mathtt{in}~N
in the context

\Gamma=(x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}})\parallel(y_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}).

Rule (
let-
\|
) is not applicable because
x_{2}
cannot be
put in parallel to
x_{1}
.
Rule (
let-
,
) is not directly applicable to
context
\Gamma
, but we have to decompose it to obtain a more
restrictive context

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}})\qquad\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}})\parallel(y_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}).

The problem is that
N
is now typechecked in context

z\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(z\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}),((x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}})\parallel(y_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}))

so that we get an artificial sequential dependency stating that
z
is
before
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
.

We address this restriction by defining a
left context

\mathcal{L}
as follows.

\mathcal{L}

\Coloneqq

[]~\mid~\mathcal{L},\Gamma~\mid~\Gamma\parallel\mathcal{L}~\mid~\mathcal{L}\parallel\Gamma

This context pattern finds the usage prefixes of resources. In our
example, we would use
\mathcal{L}=([],x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}})\parallel(y_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}})
so that
\Gamma=\mathcal{L}[x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}]
and then we can typecheck
N

with
\Gamma=\mathcal{L}[z\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}]
, that is, without imposing additional
sequential dependencies. The resulting rule subsumes both
[let-
\|
] and [let-
,
]:

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\\
\mathcal{L}[x\mathord{:}S]\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{L}[\Gamma]\vdash\mathtt{let}\,x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}

(
let
)

Depending on the resources needed by the let header
M
, we may have
to sequentialize the incoming context as in this variation of our
example.
Consider

\mathtt{let}~z=(x_{1}\otimes y_{1})~\mathtt{in}~N
in the same context

\Gamma
as before. Before we can match with some
\mathcal{L}
, we have to
sequentialize
\Gamma
to

\Gamma^{\prime}=(x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel y_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}),(x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}})
and then we can
choose
\mathcal{L}=[],(x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel y_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}})
.
Our type system has a suitable weakening rule to allow for this
sequentialization in the context.

2.3.
Borrowing vs. Resource-passing

The operations on resources can be defined using borrows or
by passing resources explicitly.
The discussion so far concentrated on borrowing interfaces. As an
example, we consider an operation that consumes a
parameter of type
A
and a resource of type

[
m_{1}
]
to return a value of type

B
.
3
3

3

b
/
op
stands for borrowing version
of operation
op
.

⬇

b
/
op

:

A

\otimes

[

m_{1}

]

\multimap

B

A resource-passing version of the same operation consumes a resource
of type
[
m_{0}
]
and returns it in its next state

[
m_{2}
]
that “peels off” the
m_{1}
=
m
(op)
denoting the single
application of
op
from the
m_{0}
, if that is defined:

⬇

f
/
op

:

A

\otimes

[

m_{0}

]

\multimap

B

\otimes

[

m_{2}

]

Here
m_{2}
=\delta_{op}(
m_{0}
)
is the (partial) transformation of

op
on the OPM. It is partial as
op
need not be defined for all
m_{0}
.
We can transform the resource-passing interface into the
borrowing one if
\delta_{op}
can be expressed by multiplication with
an element
m_{1}
in the monoid:

m_{1}
\odot
m_{2}
\leq
m_{0}
, the side
condition for
split
.
The reverse transformation is also possible as the resource returned
from
f/op
can be considered (formally) exhausted because

m_{1}
\odot\varepsilon\leq
m_{1}
. Hence, we obtain the typing
r_{0}:[\varepsilon]
.

⬇

let

(
b

\otimes

r2
)

=

f
/
op

(
a

\otimes

r
)

in

…

—-
\rightarrow

let

(
r1

\odot

r2
)

=

split@m1

r

in

let

b

=

b
/
op

(
a

\otimes

r1
)

in

…

⬇

let

b

=

b
/
op

(
a

\otimes

r
)

in

…

—-
\rightarrow

let

(
b

\otimes

r0
)

=

f
/
op

(
a

\otimes

r
)

in

let

()

=

drop

r0

in

…

2.4.
Aliasing and Permissions

Garcia et al. (2014, page 5)
use access
permissions to manage the interaction of typestate and
borrows in their language FT. Their motivating discussion involves the
following function in the setting of an API similar to the file handles from the introduction.

⬇

void

m
(
full

OF

>>

full

CF

f
,

full

OF

>>

full

OF

g
)

{

/*

FT

code

*/

f
.
close
();

print
(
g
.
file_desc
.
pos
);

}

This function takes two file handles
f
and
g
; it
closes the handle
f
first, and then obtains information from

g
. FT’s parameter notation
S >> T f
says that
f
is expected
to be in typestate
S
when called and in typestate
T

when
m
returns. Here
OF
and
CF
are short
for
OpenFile
and
ClosedFile
. The qualifier

full
indicates that the
reference must have exclusive write permission. This
function type avoids calls with aliased parameters as in

m(h, h)
. Such a call would not be safe as the function
cannot obtain information from a closed file. In FT, a
full

permission cannot split in two
full
permissions, so that it is impossible to have two
references with full permission to the same object.

In our setting and assuming that
file_desc
.
pos
is a read
operation, the function type would be

⬇

void

m
(
f

:

Handle
[

c

]

\odot

g

:

Handle
[

r

])

Suppose we wish to pass some
h

:

Handle
[

rc

]
to both
parameters of
m
. To this end, we would have to
split
h
at
r
into an ordered pair:

⬇

let

(
h1

\odot

h2
)

=

split@r
/
c

h

in

…

In the body of the let, we have that
h1

:

Handle
[

r

]
,
h2

:

Handle
[

c

]

and elimination of the ordered pair prescribes that
h1
and

h2
must be consumed in this order. In particular, we
cannot
pass them
in the reverse order to
m
.

However, if we flip the parameters and the statements in the function
body, we obtain a function that we can call with the above split of
h
:

⬇

void

ma
(
g

:

Handle
[

r

]

\odot

f

:

Handle
[

c

])

{

print
(
g
.
file_desc
.
pos
);

f
.
close
();

}

This function can be called both with aliased or unaliased resources.

To obtain the same result in FT, we would have to revise the function
signature using the read-only permission
pure
:

⬇

void

ma
(
pure

OF

>>

pure

CF

g
,

full

OF

>>

full

CF

f
)

/*

FT

code

*/

This FT signature faces a dilemma. If we use
pure CF
as the type of
g
after
the call, then we cannot call the function with unaliased arguments. If
we use
pure OF
as the after-type of
g
, we cannot
call
ma
with aliased arguments.

3.
Language

In this section, we formalize a simply typed lambda calulus with
resource operations, ordered pair and function types, and
call-by-value evaluation. Compared to the linear calculus in the
informal presentation (
Section

2
), the calculus has an
unrestricted fragment with values that can be freely duplicated and
discarded.

Our formal semantics for resource operations does not perform actual
effects (like manipulating files, etc), it just tracks the trace of
operations as an element of an OPM and blocks if an operation is not
admitted by the specification. Consequently, our type soundness result is
actually trace soundness, that is, operations only occur in an order
admitted by their specification.

3.1.
Syntax

c

\Coloneqq

\mathtt{unit}\mid\mathtt{new}_{m}\mid\mathtt{op}_{m}\mid\mathtt{split}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\mid\mathtt{drop}

M,N

\Coloneqq

c\mid l\mid x\mid\lambda x.M\mid\lambda^{\circ}x.M\mid\lambda^{>}x.M\mid\lambda^{<}x.M\mid M\,N\mid M{}^{\circ}N\mid M{}^{>}N\mid M{}^{<}N\mid M\otimes N\mid M\odot N\mid\mathtt{let}\,x\otimes y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N\mid\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N

Figure 3
.
Syntax of expressions

Figure

3
defines the syntax, where elements of an OPM are
ranged over by
m
,
constants
by
c
,
expressions
by
M
and
N
,
locations
by
l
, and
variables
by
x
and
y
.

Expressions include constants, locations, variables, abstractions, applications, and constructors and destructors for pairs.
Constants consist of
\mathtt{unit}
, the unique value of the type

\mathtt{Unit}
, and resource managing functions:
\mathtt{new}_{m}
to create a
new resource whose usage is specified by
m
,
\mathtt{op}_{m}
to
perform the operation denoted by
m
on a resource,
\mathtt{split}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
to split the usage of a given resource into the head part

m_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and the rest part
m_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and
\mathtt{drop}
to dispose of a resource.

Locations, which only occur at run time, designate a resource.
We always refer to a resource via its location from a program, meaning
the resource managing functions operate on locations.
Abstractions vary in how they capture resources.
A
non-capture abstraction

\lambda x.M
captures no resources.
On the other hand,
capture abstractions
,
\lambda^{\circ}x.M
,

\lambda^{>}x.M
, and
\lambda^{<}x.M
, may capture resources.
All abstractions bind variable
x
with scope
M
.
The superscripts of
\lambda
in capture abstractions specify a resource usage order between the resources in a given argument and those captured.
An
unordered capture abstraction

\lambda^{\circ}x.M
has no order between them.
A
right ordered capture abstraction

\lambda^{>}x.M
consumes
captured resources first and then uses those of the argument.
Conversely, a
left ordered capture abstraction

\lambda^{<}x.M
consumes resources in the argument first.
Each abstraction comes with a corresponding applications
M\,N
,

M{}^{\circ}N
,
M{}^{>}N
, and
M{}^{<}N
, that is,
M{}^{\circ}N

expects evaluating
M
results in
\lambda^{\circ}x.M_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, and so on.

Pairs vary in whether elements have a usage order between them.
An
unordered pair

M\otimes N
has no usage order between the values obtained from
M
and
N
.
A typical situation is that either or both values have no resources, but it is also possible that both have resources that are independent.
An
ordered pair

M\odot N
, on the other hand, has a usage order.
We need to consume the resources in the value obtained from
M
before those from
N
.
There are two corresponding elimination constructs
\mathtt{let}\,x\otimes y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N
and
\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N
that decompose the pair obtained
from
M
and bind
x
and
y
with scope
N
accordingly.
Elimination of the unordered pair imposes no restriction on uses of

x
and
y
in
N
, but for the ordered pair
x
must
be consumed before
y
.

Remark 3.1 (Let expressions).

We can derive several
let
expressions with different constraints on resource usage, which appear as typing constraints.
Here are some of the derived
let
expressions.

\displaystyle\mathtt{let}\,x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N\mathrel{\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}}(\lambda^{\circ}x.N){}^{\circ}M\qquad\mathtt{let}^{>}x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N\mathrel{\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}}(\lambda^{<}x.N){}^{<}M

The first one corresponds to the typing rule (
let-
\parallel
), and the second one to (
let-
,
) in
Section

2.2
.

3.2.
Operational Semantics

We define the operational semantics as a small-step reduction relation
between
configurations
—pairs of expressions and

heaps
.

Definition 3.2 (Heap).

A
heap
,
\mathcal{H}
, is a finite map from locations
to triples
(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)
, where
n
is a reference count
(with
0
denoting one reference),
m_{{\mathrm{0}}}
is the usage of the
resource specified at its creation, and
m
records the trace of operations
applied to the resource so far.
We write
\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
for the domain of
\mathcal{H}

and
\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
for the updated heap that maps

l
to
(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)
and other
l^{\prime}\neq l
to
\mathcal{H}(l^{\prime})
.

V

\Coloneqq

c\mid l\mid\lambda x.M\mid\lambda^{\circ}x.M\mid\lambda^{>}x.M\mid\lambda^{<}x.M\mid V_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes V_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mid V_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot V_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\mathcal{E}

\Coloneqq

[]\mid\mathcal{E}\,M\mid V\,\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}{}^{\circ}M\mid V{}^{\circ}\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}{}^{>}M\mid V{}^{>}\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}{}^{<}V\mid M{}^{<}\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}\otimes M\mid V\otimes\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}\odot M\mid V\odot\mathcal{E}\mid\mathtt{let}\,x\otimes y=\mathcal{E}\,\mathtt{in}\,M\mid\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=\mathcal{E}\,\mathtt{in}\,M

Figure 4
.
Values and evaluation contexts

We use a call-by-value strategy for the semantics, which is organized
by using
values
, ranged over by
V
, and
evaluation contexts
,
\mathcal{E}
, shown in
Figure

4
.
Values are a subset of expressions comprising constants, locations, abstractions, and pairs of values.
An evaluation context is an expression with one
hole

[]
.
The hole specifies the position of the sub-expression of an expression to be evaluated in the next step.
The evaluation order is generally left-to-right except for left
application, namely
\mathcal{E}{}^{<}V
and
M{}^{<}\mathcal{E}
, which is evaluated right-to-left.

As usual, we write
\mathcal{E}[M]
for the expression obtained by filling
the hole in
\mathcal{E}
with
M

and
M[V/x]
for the expression obtained by substituting

V
for
x
in
M
.

M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}

(\lambda x.M)\,V\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V/x]

[RE-Beta]

(\lambda^{\circ}x.M){}^{\circ}V\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V/x]

[RE-UBeta]

(\lambda^{>}x.M){}^{>}V\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V/x]

[RE-RBeta]

(\lambda^{<}x.M){}^{<}V\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V/x]

[RE-LBeta]

x\neq y

\mathtt{let}\,x\otimes y=V_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes V_{{\mathrm{2}}}\,\mathtt{in}\,M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V_{{\mathrm{1}}}/x][V_{{\mathrm{2}}}/y]

[RE-ULet]

x\neq y

\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=V_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot V_{{\mathrm{2}}}\,\mathtt{in}\,M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M[V_{{\mathrm{1}}}/x][V_{{\mathrm{2}}}/y]

[RE-OLet]

M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

l\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})

\mathtt{new}_{m}\,\mathtt{unit}\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(0,m,\varepsilon)\}

[RC-Ne]

There exists
m^{\prime\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}\odot m\odot m^{\prime\prime}\leq m_{{\mathrm{0}}}

\mathtt{op}_{m}\,l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m^{\prime})\}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m^{\prime}\odot m)\}

[RC-Op]

\mathtt{drop}\,l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n+1,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}\mathtt{unit}\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}

[RC-Cl1]

l\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})

m\leq m_{{\mathrm{0}}}

\mathtt{drop}\,l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(0,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}\mathtt{unit}\mid\mathcal{H}

[RC-Cl2]

\left(\begin{minipage}[243.93486pt]\raggedright\noindent$\mathtt{split}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\,l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}l\odot l\mid\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n+1,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}$\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}\right)

[RC-Sp]

M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}

\mathcal{E}[M]\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}[M^{\prime}]\mid\mathcal{H}

[R-Exp]

M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

\mathcal{E}[M]\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}[M^{\prime}]\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}

[R-Cfg]

Figure 5
.
Operational semantics

We define the semantics by three relations:
expression reduction
, denoted by
M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}
,
configuration reduction
, denoted by
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, and
contextual reduction
, denoted by
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Figure

5
contains their defining rules.

The expression reduction
M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}
states that
M
reduces
M^{\prime}
.
The rules consist of the usual
\beta
-reduction for applications and
let
-reduction for pair eliminations.
One thing we need to care about is consistency: the kind of
application and the abstraction in its function part and the kind of
let-expression and the pair in its decomposed part have to agree.

The configuration reduction
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
organizes
resource manipulation. It reads: expression
M
steps to
M^{\prime}
under the heap
\mathcal{H}
, and the heap is changed into
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.
Rule
(RC-Ne)
picks a fresh location
l
, returns it, and
adds the map from
l
to the triple
(0,m,\varepsilon)
to the
current heap: the reference counter is initialized to
0
and the
operation trace to
\varepsilon
as no operations have been performed, yet.
Rule
(RC-Op)
implements the resource-passing interface: it appends the new operation to the trace for the resource at the specified location and returns the same location.
From the heap we know that the resource was allocated with behavior

m_{{\mathrm{0}}}
and uses up to now accumulate to
m^{\prime}
. The side condition
checks if the operation

m
is admissibile: the extension
m^{\prime}\odot m
must be defined and
it must be possible to extend
m^{\prime}\odot m
to
m_{{\mathrm{0}}}
by some

m^{\prime\prime}
.

The rules
(RC-Cl1)
and
(RC-Cl2)
dispose of the location and return
\mathtt{unit}
.
Rule
(RC-Cl1)
is for a location with an active alias in the
program, as seen from the reference count.
So, it just decrements the reference counter and does not dispose of the resource itself.
Rule
(RC-Cl2)
applies to the last location pointing to a resource.
So,
(RC-Cl2)
removes the resource from the heap, with the side
condition checking that the resource is fully used up, that is, the recorded operations belong to the original usage.
The premise
l\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
in
(RC-Cl2)
prevents the
resulting heap from having a resource at the disposed location.
At this point we can recover the borrowing interface to resources as it is
defineable by
i/op M
=\mathtt{drop}\,(\mathtt{op}_{m}\,M)
.

Rule
(RC-Sp)
duplicates a location, increments the reference counter of the resource pointed to by the location, and returns the duplicated locations as a pair.
The reader may wonder why the rule does not touch the resource usage, although we have explained that
\mathtt{split}_{m,m^{\prime}}\,l
splits the resource usage for
l
by
m
and
m^{\prime}
.
In
Section

3.3
, we will see that we manage the resource usage solely by the type of the location.
Each of the three occurrences of
l
in the expression may have a
different type, but this fact is not visible in the operational rule.

Contextual reduction
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
defines reduction for
a whole program by reverting to either expression reduction or configuration
reduction in a suitable evaluation context.

3.3.
Type System

The primary objective of the type system is to prevent any operation
on a resource that would violate the usage specification at its creation.
This property is crucial for type safety—a well-typed program does not
get stuck—since
(RC-Op)
and
(RC-Cl2)
expect that
resources are used according to their specification.

e

\Coloneqq

0\mid 1\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

T,S

\Coloneqq

\mathtt{Unit}\mid[m]\mid S\rightarrow_{e}T\mid S\rightarrowtriangle_{e}T\mid S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T\mid S\rightarrowtail_{e}T\mid S\otimes T\mid S\odot T

Figure 6
.
Syntax of types

Figure

6
defines the syntax of types, where
e
ranges
over
effects
and
T
and
S
over
types
.
Effects are a crude way of keeping track whether resource operations might
happen. The effect
0
indicates that there are no operations;
effect
1
indicates that an operation might happen; effect
composition
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
just takes the maximum of
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

The unit type
\mathtt{Unit}
is the type for
\mathtt{unit}
.
A trace type
[m]
classifies a resource location, which is good
for operations according to
m
.
As customary in an ordered type system, there are several arrow types
corresponding to the different kinds of abstraction: that is,
S\rightarrow_{e}T
for non-capture abstractions;
S\rightarrowtriangle_{e}T
for unordered (linear)
capture abstractions;
S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T
for right ordered capture
abstractions; and
S\rightarrowtail_{e}T
for left ordered capture
abstractions.
In each case,
S
is the parameter type,
T
is the return
type, and the subscript
e
denotes the latent effect that happens when the abstraction is invoked.
The last two types are product types:
S\otimes T
for unordered pairs
and
S\odot T
for ordered pairs, where
S
and
T
are the
types of the first and second projection.

Each type is classified as either
unrestricted
or

ordered
, where an unrestricted type does not contain a
resource, a linear function, or an ordered function. For that reason,
values of unrestricted type can be safely duplicated.

Definition 3.3 (Unrestricted and ordered types).

We call
T

unrestricted
(
ordered
) if and only if
\mathtt{unr}\,T
(
\mathtt{ord}\,T
) is derivable by the following rules, respectively.

\mathtt{unr}\,\mathtt{Unit}

\mathtt{unr}\,(S\rightarrow_{e}T)

\mathtt{unr}\,S

\mathtt{unr}\,T

\mathtt{unr}\,(S\otimes T)

\mathtt{unr}\,S

\mathtt{unr}\,T

\mathtt{unr}\,(S\odot T)

\mathtt{ord}\,[m]

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\rightarrowtriangle_{e}T)

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T)

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\rightarrowtail_{e}T)

\mathtt{ord}\,S

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\otimes T)

\mathtt{ord}\,T

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\otimes T)

\mathtt{ord}\,S

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\odot T)

\mathtt{ord}\,T

\mathtt{ord}\,(S\odot T)

3.3.1.
Typing Contexts

A novel part of our type system is the formulation of
typing contexts
.
We define the syntax of typing contexts as follows, where

bindings
are ranged over by
b
, typing contexts by
\Gamma
, and
context patterns
by
\mathcal{G}
.

\displaystyle b\Coloneqq x\mathord{:}T~\mid~l\mathord{:}[m]\qquad\Gamma\Coloneqq\cdot~\mid~b~\mid~\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}~\mid~\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\qquad\mathcal{G}\Coloneqq[]~\mid~\Gamma,\mathcal{G}~\mid~\mathcal{G},\Gamma~\mid~\Gamma\parallel\mathcal{G}~\mid~\mathcal{G}\parallel\Gamma

A typing context assigns the type of each variable in an expression by a
variable binding

x\mathord{:}T
and each location by a
location binding

l\mathord{:}[m]
during typing.
The other context formers have been introduced in
Section

2.2
.
A typing context also represents the usage count and order of variables and locations.
For instance, the same variable may be bound several times and the contexts
(x\mathord{:}T,x\mathord{:}T)
and
(x\mathord{:}T)
are different.

Definition 3.4 (Notations for typing contexts).

The
domain
of
\Gamma
is the set of variables and locations bound in
\Gamma
, denoted by
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
.
We write
\#_{b}(\Gamma)
for the number of occurences of
b
in
\Gamma
.
We write
b\in\Gamma
if and only if
\#_{b}(\Gamma)>0
.
We write
\Gamma[\Gamma^{\prime}/b]
to express the typing context obtained
from
\Gamma
by replacing all bindings
b
in
\Gamma
with

\Gamma^{\prime}
and we write
\Gamma^{-b}
if
\Gamma^{\prime}=\cdot
.
We lift the notions of unrestricted and ordered types to bindings, denoted by
\mathtt{unr}\,b
and
\mathtt{ord}\,b
, according to the type part of a binding.
Furthermore, we lift the notion unrestricted to typing contexts, denoted by
\mathtt{unr}\,\Gamma
, meaning that
\mathtt{unr}\,b
for all
b\in\Gamma
.
We use the same notations for context patterns

\mathcal{G}
, mutatis mutandis.

As we have seen, a typing context may have multiple bindings for the same variable.
However, we do not use arbitrary typing contexts but only
well-formed
ones.

Definition 3.5 (Well-formed typing context).

We call
\Gamma

well-formed
if and only if

•

S=T
for any
x\mathord{:}S\in\Gamma
and
x\mathord{:}T\in\Gamma
, and

•

\#_{x\mathord{:}T}(\Gamma)\leq 1
for any
x
and
\mathtt{ord}\,T
.

Informally,
\Gamma
is well-formed if and only if each variable is assigned a unique type, and only unrestricted bindings can have multiple occurrences.
This definition respects the meaning of unrestricted and ordered types.
Ordered bindings can occur at most once.

3.3.2.
Semantics of Typing Contexts

Many syntactically different typing contexts have the same meaning—for instance
\Gamma,\cdot
and
\Gamma
.
To precisely capture the meaning of a typing context, we interpret a typing context as a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of ordered bindings and a set of unrestricted bindings.
In the graph, vertices represent bindings and edges represent the ordering constraints among the bindings.
This structure captures the strength of an ordering constraint as the number of edges in the graph interpretation.
Using the interpretation, we can define equivalence and a subcontext relation between typing contexts semantically.

We start by recalling some definitions from graph theory.

Definition 3.6 (Graph representation).

A
graph representation
, denoted by
\mathfrak{G}
, is a triple

(n,v,E)
, where
n\geq 0
is the number of
vertices in the graph,

v
is a map from
\mathbb{N}_{<n}

to bindings labeling each vertex by a binding, and
E
is a binary
relation between
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
and
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
representing the
directed edges of the graph.
As we mentioned, a graph representation must be acyclic.

\displaystyle(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})*(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})

\displaystyle=(n_{1}+n_{2},v,E_{1}\cup E_{2}^{\prime}\cup E^{\prime}),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle v(n)

\displaystyle=v_{1}(n)\quad\text{if $0\leq n<n_{1}$}\quad\text{and}\quad v_{2}(n-n_{1})\quad\text{if $n_{1}\leq n<n_{1}+n_{2}$}

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\,(n_{1}+n,n_{1}+m)\mid(n,m)\in E_{2}\,\}

\displaystyle E^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\,(n,n_{1}+m)\mid 0\leq n<n_{1},0\leq m<n_{2}\,\}

\displaystyle(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})\cup(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})

\displaystyle=(n_{1}+n_{2},v,E_{1}\cup E_{2}^{\prime}),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle v(n)

\displaystyle=v_{1}(n)\quad\text{if $0\leq n<n_{1}$}\quad\text{and}\quad v_{2}(n-n_{1})\quad\text{if $n_{1}\leq n<n_{1}+n_{2}$}

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{\,(n_{1}+n,n_{1}+m)\mid(n,m)\in E_{2}\,\}

Figure 7
.
Join and union of graph representations

Definition 3.7 (Join and union of graph representations).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
.
The join
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and the union
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are defined in
Figure

7
.

Definition 3.8 (Isomorphic graph representation).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n,v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n,v_{2},E_{2})
.
We call
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}

isomorphic
and write
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if and only if there is a bijection
f
from
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
to
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
satisfying
E_{1}=\{(f(n),f(m))\mid(n,m)\in E_{2}\}
and
v_{1}\circ f=v_{2}
.

Definition 3.9 (Spanning graph representations).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n,v,E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n,v,E_{2})
.
We call
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
a
spanning graph representation
of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, denoted by
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, if and only if
E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}
.

Definition 3.10 (Topological ordering of a graph representation).

A
topological ordering
of
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
is a bijection
f
from
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
to
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
satisfying
f^{-1}(n_{1})<f^{-1}(n_{2})
for all
(n_{1},n_{2})\in E
.
The bijective function defines the ordering of the vertices of the representation.

Now, we define the interpretation of a typing context and then define
equivalence and the subcontext relation using the interpretation.

Definition 3.11 (Context interpretation).

We define the
interpretation
of
\Gamma
, denoted by

[\![\Gamma]\!]
, as a pair of a graph and a set of unrestricted bindings.

\displaystyle[\![\cdot]\!]

\displaystyle=(0,\emptyset,\emptyset);\emptyset

\displaystyle[\![b]\!]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(0,\emptyset,\emptyset);\{b\}&\text{if $\mathtt{unr}\,b$}\\
(1,\{0\mapsto b\},\emptyset);\emptyset&\text{if $\mathtt{ord}\,b$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\qquad\text{where}\quad\text{$[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{1}}}$ and $[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{2}}}$}

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\qquad\text{where}\quad\text{$[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{1}}}$ and $[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};\mathbb{S}_{{\mathrm{2}}}$}

Definition 3.12 (Equivalent typing contexts).

We say
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is equivalent to
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and write
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if and only if
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\wedge S_{1}=S_{2}
for
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}};S_{1}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};S_{2}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.

In
Section

2.2
we said that the context formers

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are monoids with unit
\cdot
and that
unordered composition is commutative. Moreover, ordered composition
with an unrestricted binding
b
is the same as unordered
composition with
b
(demotion). The graph interpretation of a
context yields a semantic characterization of these algebraic laws.

Proposition 3.13.

Contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are related by applying the monoid laws,
commutativity of unordered composition, and demotion with
unrestricted bindings, if and only if
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.

Definition 3.14 (Subcontexts).

We say
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is a subcontext of
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and write
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if and only if there exist
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sim\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\sim\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\wedge S_{1}\supseteq S_{2}
for
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}};S_{1}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};S_{2}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.

The subcontext relation
adds more ordering constraints between the bindings in a typing context.
If
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, a typing under
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is more constrained on variables and locations use.
So, we design our type system so that an expression is typeable under
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
if we can type an expression under
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
The spanning condition characterizes the additional edges that
sequentialize more bindings.

The subcontext relation cannot be used like classic weakening. For
instance, we will see that we can apply rule
[T-UPair]
to construct

x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes x_{{\mathrm{2}}}
given the context
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}
. But this is
not possible in the subcontext
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Unrestricted bindings enjoy all substructural rules—exchange,
weakening, and contraction—, which is managed by their treatment as
a set in the interpretation.

3.3.3.
Typing Rules

\Gamma\vdash M:T\mid e

\cdot\vdash\mathtt{unit}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 0

[T-Unit]

\cdot\vdash\mathtt{new}_{m}:\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrow_{0}[m]\mid 0

[T-New]

m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{0}}}

\cdot\vdash\mathtt{op}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}}}:[m_{{\mathrm{0}}}]\rightarrow_{1}[m_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\mid 0

[T-Op]

m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{0}}}

\cdot\vdash\mathtt{split}_{m_{{\mathrm{1}}},m_{{\mathrm{2}}}}:[m_{{\mathrm{0}}}]\rightarrow_{0}([m_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\odot[m_{{\mathrm{2}}}])\mid 0

[T-Split]

\varepsilon\leq m

\cdot\vdash\mathtt{drop}:[m]\rightarrow_{0}\mathtt{Unit}\mid 0

[T-Drop]

x\mathord{:}T\vdash x:T\mid 0

[T-Var]

l\mathord{:}[m]\vdash l:[m]\mid 0

[T-Loc]

x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\mathtt{unr}\,\Gamma

\Gamma,x\mathord{:}S\vdash M:T\mid e

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:S\rightarrow_{e}T\mid 0

[T-Abs]

x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\Gamma\parallel x\mathord{:}S\vdash M:T\mid e

\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{\circ}x.M:S\rightarrowtriangle_{e}T\mid 0

[T-UAbs]

x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

\Gamma,x\mathord{:}S\vdash M:T\mid e

\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{>}x.M:S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T\mid 0

[T-RAbs]

x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)

x\mathord{:}S,\Gamma\vdash M:T\mid e

\Gamma\vdash\lambda^{<}x.M:S\rightarrowtail_{e}T\mid 0

[T-LAbs]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\rightarrow_{e}T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M\,N:T\mid e\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-App]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\rightarrowtriangle_{e}T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M{}^{\circ}N:T\mid e\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-UApp]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:S\mid 0

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M{}^{>}N:T\mid e\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

[T-RApp]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M:S\rightarrowtail_{e}T\mid 0

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash N:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M{}^{<}N:T\mid e\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-LApp]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M\otimes N:S\otimes T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-UPair]

\mathtt{ord}\,S
implies
e_{{\mathrm{2}}}=0

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M\odot N:S\odot T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-OPair]

\{x\}\uplus\{y\}\uplus\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{G}[\Gamma])

\Gamma\vdash M:S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\otimes S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mid 0

\mathcal{G}[x\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel y\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\vdash N:T\mid e

\mathcal{G}[\Gamma]\vdash\mathtt{let}\,x\otimes y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N:T\mid e

[T-ULet]

\{x\}\uplus\{y\}\uplus\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{G}[\Gamma])

\Gamma\vdash M:S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot S_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mid 0

\mathcal{G}[x\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{1}}},y\mathord{:}S_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\vdash N:T\mid e

\mathcal{G}[\Gamma]\vdash\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N:T\mid e

[T-OLet]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\lesssim\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}

e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash M:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[T-Weaken]

Figure 8
.
Typing rules

The typing judgment
\Gamma\vdash M:T\mid e
says that
M
has type

T
under context
\Gamma
with effect
e
. The judgment is
defined by the inference rules in
Figure

8
.

The typing rules for constants reflect their semantics and have no effect.
A constant
\mathtt{op}_{m}
only has an effect on its application as
indicated by the subscript
1
of the arrow.
As explained earlier, we only consider resource operations as an
effect; effects are not related to heap modifications.
The typing rules for variables and locations simply pick the type in the typing context.
Rule
(T-Weaken)
enables us to weaken the typing context
according to the subcontext relation and to weaken the effect (a
standard treatment for subeffecting).

The most intriguing rules are ones for abstractions and applications,
which control the usage order of resources that do not follow the
syntactic order.
Rule
(T-Abs)
handles non-capture abstractions, which must not capture a resource.
The premise
\mathtt{unr}\,\Gamma
expresses this condition.
The parameter type
S
can be ordered as an actual parameter is passed from outside.
The corresponding application rule is
(T-App)
.
The typing context of the conclusion is supposed to be
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and
M
is typed under
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
N
is typed under
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
because
M
is evaluated first.
The function part and the argument part can have an effect, as neither
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
nor
e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is constrained.
The argument part can have an effect because the function part becomes a non-capture abstraction; that means all usages assigned for
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
are consumed, and thus,
N
can perform operations on resources in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

In the typing rules for capture abstractions,
\Gamma
may contain
ordered bindings, but the specific rules for concatenation of the binding for the parameter differs for each abstraction.
The difference affects how the typing context is split in the corresponding application rule.
For example, the typing environment of rule
(T-UApp)
is supposed to be
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, which corresponds to the typing environment
\Gamma\parallel x\mathord{:}S
of the premise in
(T-UAbs)
.
This correspondence maintains unconsumed resources in the argument part to fit into the original usage position when an application happens.
Another remark is the effects of each subexpression.
In rule
(T-UApp)
, both subexpressions can have an effect.
The evaluation of
M
may not consume resources in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
fully,
which are then captured by the resulting abstraction, but
N
can still use a resource in
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
because
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are order independent.
On the other hand, one of the subexpressions in rules
(T-RApp)

and
(T-LApp)
must not have an effect because, in the rule,

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
has an ordering constraint.
We also note that
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are swapped in the premises of
rule
(T-LApp)
, which reflects the evaluation order of the left application.

The typing rules for pairs are organized similarly to abstractions and applications.
Regarding the elimination rules—
(T-ULet)
and

(T-OLet)
, we do not consider variations in how to introduce
the bound variables for the typing context for
N
, which we can
have, but it can be handled as explained in

Section

2.2
.

Example 3.15.

The following are derivations for the desugaring of
let
-expressions presented in
Remark

3.1
, which clarify the typing rules for them (we have anticipated it in
Section

2.2
).

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel x\mathord{:}S\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

(T-UAbs)

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\lambda^{\circ}x.N:S\rightarrowtriangle_{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}T\mid 0

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

(T-UApp)
and
(T-Weaken)

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\lambda^{\circ}x.N){}^{\circ}M:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

x\mathord{:}S,\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash N:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

(T-LAbs)

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash\lambda^{<}x.N:S\rightarrowtail_{e_{{\mathrm{2}}}}T\mid 0

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash M:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

(T-LApp)

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\vdash(\lambda^{<}x.N){}^{<}M:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}

Example 3.16.

Let’s examine typing for the file permission example introduced in
Example

2.2
on the formal calculus.
Consider the following typeable expression, where
M;N
is the abbreviation of
\mathtt{let}^{>}x=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N
for some fresh
x
.
This expression creates the
thunk

f
that keeps
read
operation on the opened file and uses it after that.
So, we need to take care of the usage order of
f
, too.

\displaystyle\texttt{let}\,x=\texttt{new}_{(r|w)^{*}c}\,\mathtt{unit}\,\texttt{in}

\displaystyle\texttt{let}\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot x_{{\mathrm{2}}}=\texttt{split}_{r,(r|w)^{*}c}\,x\,\texttt{in}

\displaystyle\texttt{let}^{>}\,f=\lambda^{\circ}z.\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{r}\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}})\,\texttt{in}

\displaystyle f^{\circ}\mathtt{unit};\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{c}\,x_{{\mathrm{2}}})

Let’s see how our type system handles the usage order of
f
.
For ease of writing, we abbreviate subexpressions as follows.

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\texttt{let}\,x=\texttt{new}_{(r|w)^{*}c}\,\mathtt{unit}\,\texttt{in}\,M_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\displaystyle M_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\texttt{let}\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot x_{{\mathrm{2}}}=\texttt{split}_{r,(r|w)^{*}c}\,x\,\texttt{in}\,M_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\displaystyle M_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq\texttt{let}^{>}\,f=\lambda^{\circ}z.\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{r}\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}})\,\texttt{in}\,M_{{\mathrm{3}}}

\displaystyle M_{{\mathrm{3}}}

\displaystyle\Coloneqq f^{\circ}\mathtt{unit};\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{c}\,x_{{\mathrm{2}}})

Then the expression is typed by the following derivations—it is still not complete, but we can fill the omitted subderivations.

f\mathord{:}\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrowtriangle_{1}\mathtt{Unit}\vdash f^{\circ}\mathtt{unit}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{c}\,x_{{\mathrm{2}}}):\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

f\mathord{:}\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrowtriangle_{1}\mathtt{Unit},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{3}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}[r]\vdash\lambda^{\circ}z.\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{r}\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}}):\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrowtriangle_{1}\mathtt{Unit}\mid 0

f\mathord{:}\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrowtriangle_{1}\mathtt{Unit},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{3}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}[r],x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

x\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash\texttt{split}_{r,(r|w)^{*}c}\,x:[r]\odot[(r|w)^{*}c]\mid 0

x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}[r],x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

x\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

\cdot\vdash\texttt{new}_{(r|w)^{*}c}\,\mathtt{unit}:[(r|w)^{*}c]\mid 0

x\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]\vdash M_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

\cdot\vdash M:\mathtt{Unit}\mid 1

The subexpression at position
M_{{\mathrm{3}}}
is typed in context
f\mathord{:}\mathtt{Unit}\rightarrowtriangle_{1}\mathtt{Unit},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}[(r|w)^{*}c]
.
The type system rejects this misuse of thunk
f
as in
\texttt{drop}\,(\texttt{op}_{c}\,x_{{\mathrm{2}}});f^{\circ}\mathtt{unit}
.

3.4.
Type Soundness

In this subsection we sketch the proof of type soundness. It is
structured in the standard way by the following two propositions,
preservation and progress, where
C
denotes a
run-time
context
, which assigns a resource usage to each label, and
C\vdash\mathcal{H}
is a
heap typing
; both are defined below.

Proposition 3.17 (Preservation).

If
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
C\vdash M:T\mid e
, and
C\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:T\mid e
and
C^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 3.18 (Progress).

If
C\vdash M:T\mid e
and
C,C^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then

(1)

M
is a value, namely
M=V
for some
V
, or

(2)

M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Roughly speaking, preservation says the usage assignment by
C
,
which changes by reducing an expression, stays correct against the
specification in the heap, and progress says that a well-typed expression does not get stuck.

We start by defining run-time contexts and heap typing.

Definition 3.19 (Run-time context).

We call a typing context
run-time context
, denoted by
C
, if and only if the typing context has no variable bindings.
We abuse
[\![C]\!]
to denote only the graph part of the interpretation because the set part is always empty.

Definition 3.20 (Run-time context pattern).

We call a typing context pattern
run-time context pattern
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}
, if and only if the pattern has no variable bindings.

Definition 3.21 (Focus on location).

A
focus
on location
l
in
C
, denoted by
\langle C\rangle_{l}
, is the run-time context obtained from
C
by replacing all bindings
l^{\prime}\mathord{:}[m]\in C
such that
l^{\prime}\neq l
with
\cdot
.
We abuse the notation for run-time context patterns as intended.

Definition 3.22 (Order-defined run-time environment).

We call
C

order-defined
if and only if a topological ordering of
[\![\langle C\rangle_{l}]\!]
is unique for any
l
.

Definition 3.23 (Usage projection).

Suppose a topological ordering of
[\![C]\!]
is unique.
We write
\overline{C}
to express
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot\dots\odot m_{k}
where
l_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}[m_{{\mathrm{1}}}],\dots,l_{k}\mathord{:}[m_{k}]
is the unique topological ordering of
[\![C]\!]
.
We typically use the projection for
\langle C\rangle_{l}
.
In such a case,
l_{{\mathrm{1}}}=\dots=l_{k}=l
.

Definition 3.24 (Heap typing).

We say
C

types

\mathcal{H}
, denoted by
C\vdash\mathcal{H}
, if and only if

•

C
is order-defined,

•

\mathtt{dom}(C)=\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
, and

•

for any
l\in\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
and
(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)=\mathcal{H}(l)
:

(1)

|[\![\langle C\rangle_{l}]\!]|_{\bullet}=n+1
,

(2)

m\odot\overline{\langle C\rangle_{l}}\leq m_{{\mathrm{0}}}
.

The first condition of heap typing says there is no ambiguity in the usage order specified by
C
.
The second condition demands strong agreement between the domain of
C
and
\mathcal{H}
, by which the type system guarantees all resources are disposed of properly.
The third condition maintains the consistency of reference countings and resource usage; that is, the number of bindings for
l
in
C
coincides with the corresponding reference count, and the multiplication of performed operations so far and remaining usage is defined and respected the original usage.

Now, we sketch the proof of type soundness by introducing important lemmas.

For the pure lambda calculus parts, we need a substitution lemma as the usual proof strategy.

Lemma 3.25 (Value substitution).

If
\mathcal{G}[x\mathord{:}S]\vdash M:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{G}[\cdot])
, and
C\vdash V:S\mid e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, then
\mathcal{G}[C]\vdash M[V/x]:T\mid e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\sqcup e_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.4
.

Using the substitution lemma, we can show the preservation for expression reduction as follows.

Lemma 3.26 (Preservation for
\longrightarrow_{\beta}
).

If
M\longrightarrow_{\beta}M^{\prime}
and
C\vdash M:T\mid e
, then
C\vdash M^{\prime}:T\mid e
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.6
.

The remaining part of the preservation is configuration reduction.
We prove each configuration reduction rule preserves the correctness as follows.
Each item tracks the change of a run-time context and a heap for each reduction rule.

Lemma 3.27.

(1)

If
\mathcal{C}[\cdot]\vdash\mathcal{H}
and
l\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
, then
\mathcal{C}[l\mathord{:}[m]]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(0,m,\varepsilon)\}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{C}[l\mathord{:}[m^{\prime}]]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
and
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m^{\prime}
,

then
\mathcal{C}[l\mathord{:}[m_{{\mathrm{1}}}],l\mathord{:}[m_{{\mathrm{2}}}]]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n+1,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
.

(3)

If
l\mathord{:}[m^{\prime}],C\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
and
m_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot m_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq m^{\prime}
,

then
l\mathord{:}[m_{{\mathrm{2}}}],C\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m\odot m_{{\mathrm{1}}})\}
.

(4)

If
\mathcal{C}[l\mathord{:}[m^{\prime}]]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n+1,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
and
\varepsilon\leq m^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{C}[\cdot]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(n,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
.

(5)

If
\mathcal{C}[l\mathord{:}[m^{\prime}]]\vdash\mathcal{H}\cup\{l\mapsto(0,m_{{\mathrm{0}}},m)\}
and
l\notin\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{H})
, then
\mathcal{C}[\cdot]\vdash\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.5
,
subsection C.5
,
subsection C.5
,
subsection C.5
, and
subsection C.5
.

To show the preservation for the configuration reduction, we show the preservation for effect-free expressions first, and then we show the preservation for arbitrary expressions.
We use the first lemma to show the second lemma.

Lemma 3.28 (Preservation for
\longrightarrow_{\gamma}
with effect-free expressions).

If
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
C\vdash M:T\mid 0
, and
\mathcal{C}[C]\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
(\mathtt{dom}(C^{\prime})\setminus\mathtt{dom}(C))\cap\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{C}[\cdot])=\emptyset
,
C^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:T\mid 0
, and
\mathcal{C}[C^{\prime}]\vdash\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.6
.

Lemma 3.29 (Preservation for
\longrightarrow_{\gamma}
).

If
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow_{\gamma}M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
C\vdash M:T\mid e
, and
C,C^{\prime\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
(\mathtt{dom}(C^{\prime})\setminus\mathtt{dom}(C))\cap\mathtt{dom}(C^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
,
C^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:T\mid e
, and
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.6
.

Summing up the preservation lemmas for expression reduction and configuration reduction, we have the preservation lemma.

Lemma 3.30 (Preservation).

If
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
,
C\vdash M:T\mid e
, and
C\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then there exists
C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:T\mid e
and
C^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.6
.

Progress can be shown rather straightforwardly.
We need to show the progress for effect-free expressions first and then the objective lemma, too.

Lemma 3.31 (Progress of effect-free expressions).

If
