C\vdash M:T\mid 0
and
\mathcal{C}[C]\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then

(1)

M
is a value, namely
M=V
for some
V
, or

(2)

for any
\mathcal{H}
,
M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.7
.

Lemma 3.32 (Progress).

If
C\vdash M:T\mid e
and
C,C^{\prime}\vdash\mathcal{H}
, then

(1)

M
is a value, namely
M=V
for some
V
, or

(2)

M\mid\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See
subsection C.7
.

We compile the preservation and progress lemma as the following single proposition.

Proposition 3.33 (Soundness).

If
\cdot\vdash M:T\mid e
and
\mathtt{unr}\,T
, then

(1)

M\mid\{\}\longrightarrow^{*}V\mid\{\}
, or

(2)

the evaluation of
M\mid\{\}
diverges.

4.
Algorithmic Typing and Implementation

The logical typing relation from

Section

3.3.3
has many rules
that are not deterministic. For example, the application rule

T-UApp
requires the typing context to be split into
two subcontexts
\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}
.
To implement a type checker, an efficient way to compute these
splittings is required.
Furthermore, the need to syntactically distinguish between terms with
different ordering constraints poses a burden on the programmer and
introduces syntactic noise, e.g., either
M\,N
or
M{}^{\circ}N
has
to be used for function application depending on whether an
unrestricted or a linear function is applied.

In this section, we present an algorithmic typing, which splits
contexts deterministically and does not require annotating terms with ordering constraints.
The algorithmic typing is formulated using bidirectional
typing
(
Pierce and Turner, 1998
)
to minimize the number of required type
annotations, and translates terms from a surface syntax to
terms in the internal syntax from
Figure

3
.

M^{\uparrow},N^{\uparrow}

\Coloneqq

\texttt{unit}\mid\mathop{\texttt{new}_{m}}\mid\mathop{\texttt{op}_{m}}M^{\uparrow}\mid\mathop{\texttt{split}_{m}}M^{\uparrow}\mid\mathop{\texttt{drop}}M^{\uparrow}

\mid

x\mid\mathop{M^{\uparrow}}{N^{\uparrow}}\mid({M^{\uparrow}}\mathop{,}{N^{\uparrow}})\mid\mathop{\texttt{let}}{x},{y}={M^{\uparrow}}\mathop{\texttt{in}}{N^{\uparrow}}\mid{M^{\downarrow}}:{T}

M^{\downarrow},N^{\downarrow}

\Coloneqq

\mathop{\lambda{x}.}M^{\downarrow}\mid M^{\uparrow}

Figure 9
.
Surface Syntax

The surface syntax is shown in
Figure

9
.
As customary with bidirectional typing,
we distinguish between
inferable
expressions
M^{\uparrow}
and

checkable
expressions
M^{\downarrow}
.
An inferable expression can also be checked, but a checkable
expression
M^{\downarrow}
can only be inferred by wrapping it in a type
annotation
{M^{\downarrow}}:{T}
.
Lambda abstractions are the only non-inferable terms, thus they require a type annotation.
Function constants related to resource management are bundled with their arguments, e.g.,
instead of the constant
\mathtt{op}_{m}
, there is the expression
\mathop{\texttt{op}_{m}}M^{\uparrow}
.
This bundling enables inferring the types of resource operations without introducing resource polymorphism.
The introduction and elimination forms of function and product types
are collapsed into unannotated terms, e.g., the surface expression
\mathop{M^{\uparrow}}{N^{\uparrow}}
represents
all four internal expressions
M\,N
,
M{}^{\circ}N
,
M{}^{>}N
, and
M{}^{<}N
.

\frac{\Gamma_{1}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({M^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma_{2}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({N^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}\quad\quad{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{S}\mid{e_{1}}\Rightarrow{M}{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{N^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e_{2}}\Rightarrow{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{({M^{\uparrow}}\mathop{,}{N^{\uparrow}})}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{(S\otimes T)}\mid{(e_{1}\mathop{\sqcup}e_{2})}\Rightarrow{(M\otimes N)}}\quad\textsc{AT-UPair}\frac{\Gamma\lesssim x:T}{{\Gamma}\vdash{x}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{0}\Rightarrow{x}}\quad\textsc{AT-Var}\frac{\Gamma_{1}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({M^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma_{2}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({N^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{,}\Gamma_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma\not\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}\mathop{\texttt{ord}}{S}\texttt{ implies }e_{2}=0\quad\quad{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{S}\mid{e_{1}}\Rightarrow{M}{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{N^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e_{2}}\Rightarrow{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{({M^{\uparrow}}\mathop{,}{N^{\uparrow}})}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{(S\odot T)}\mid{(e_{1}\mathop{\sqcup}e_{2})}\Rightarrow{(M\odot N)}}\quad\textsc{AT-OPair}\frac{\Gamma\lesssim\cdot}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\texttt{unit}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{\mathtt{Unit}}\mid{0}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{unit}}}\quad\textsc{AT-Unit}\frac{{\Gamma}\mathop{\Downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({M^{\uparrow}})}}=(\mathcal{G},\Gamma^{\prime})\quad\{x\}\uplus\{y\}\uplus\mathtt{dom}(\mathcal{G}[\Gamma^{\prime}])\quad\quad{\Gamma^{\prime}}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{(S_{{\mathrm{1}}}\odot S_{{\mathrm{2}}})}\mid{0}\Rightarrow{M}{\mathcal{G}[x:S_{1}\mathop{,}y:S_{2}]}\vdash{N^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathop{\texttt{let}}{x},{y}={M^{\uparrow}}\mathop{\texttt{in}}{N^{\uparrow}}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{(\mathtt{let}\,x\odot y=M\,\mathtt{in}\,N)}}\quad\textsc{AT-OLet}

Figure 10
.
Algorithmic Typing (Selected Rules)

Figure

10
shows selected rules from the algorithmic typing.
Having decidability in mind, we think of the checking relation

{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\downarrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\downarrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}
as taking
\Gamma
,
M^{\downarrow}
, and
T
as inputs
and producing
e
and
M
as outputs, and of the inference relation

{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}
as taking
\Gamma
and
M^{\uparrow}
as inputs
and producing
T
,
e
, and
M
as outputs.

The
AT-UPair
rule describes how to infer the type of an unordered pair.
Here the input context
\Gamma
needs to be rearranged into
two parallel subcontexts, i.e.
\Gamma\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}
,
which are used to infer the types of the subexpressions.
As each subexpression requires bindings for precisely its free variables, we can compute the subcontexts
by restricting
\Gamma
to the free variables of the respective subexpression.
This is achieved with the
context restriction
function
{\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}}
, which replaces
each variable binding
x:T
in
\Gamma
, where
x\not\in X
, with the empty context.
To check that
\Gamma
indeed decomposes to
\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}
, we require

\Gamma_{1}\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}
, which is efficiently decidable.

The
AT-OPair
rule describes how to infer the type of an ordered pair.
It is analogous to
AT-UPair
, but additionally requires
\Gamma\not\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}
.
This way, typing is deterministic and a pair expression is only typed as an ordered pair
if it cannot be typed as an unordered pair.

The
AT-OLet
rule describes inference for elimination of ordered pairs.
Here the input context
\Gamma
needs to rearranged into a subcontext
and a context pattern,
i.e.,
\Gamma\lesssim\mathcal{G}[\Gamma^{\prime}]
, where

\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}
are used to infer the types of the first and
second subexpression, respectively.
Ideally, we would like to compute
\Gamma^{\prime}
using context restriction
as shown for the
AT-UPair
rule, and compute
\mathcal{G}
by
replacing the occurence of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Gamma
with a hole.
However,
\Gamma^{\prime}
does not need to occur in
\Gamma
as a whole,
but the bindings of
\Gamma^{\prime}
could be interleaved with other bindings.
Consequently, the task at hand is to figure out if
\Gamma
can be rearranged
to some context
\overline{\Gamma}
with
\Gamma\lesssim\overline{\Gamma}

such that
\overline{\Gamma}
contains the bindings for the free variables of the first
subexpression as one subcontext.
This is achieved with the
context decomposition
function

{\Gamma}\mathop{\Downarrow_{X}}
, which either fails or returns some
\mathcal{G}
and

\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma\lesssim\mathcal{G}[\Gamma^{\prime}]
,

\texttt{dom}({\Gamma^{\prime}})\subseteq X
, and
X
is disjoint from the variables
with ordered types in
\mathcal{G}
.
Essentially, this function systematically applies context isomorphisms
\simeq

and the subcontext rule

(\Gamma_{1}\mathop{,}\Gamma_{2})\mathop{\|}(\Gamma_{3}\mathop{,}\Gamma_{4})\lesssim(\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{3})\mathop{,}(\Gamma_{2}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{4})

to separate bindings with variables in
X
from other bindings, and ensures
that unrestricted bindings appear both in
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

The
AT-Var
and
AT-Unit
rules describe how to infer
the types of variables and the
unit
constant.
These expressions are leaves in the syntax tree, so we need to ensure
that no restricted variables are dropped
by requiring

\Gamma\lesssim x:T
for variables
x
, and

\Gamma\lesssim\cdot
for
unit
.

The rules discussed above are representative, and the omitted rules
work similarly.
The formal definition of context restriction, context decomposition,
and the omitted typing rules can be found in the supplementary
material and the Agda formalization.

4.1.
Properties

We have proved decidability of algorithmic typing and soundness with
respect to the logical typing in the theorem prover
Agda
(
Norell, 2009
)
.

Theorem 4.1 (Soundness).

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}

or
{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\downarrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\downarrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}

then
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{T}\mid{e}
.

Proof.

Mutual rule induction on the typing derivations.
A detailed proof can be found in our Agda formalization
in the file
LawAndOrder/Algorithmic/TypingSound.agda
.
∎

Decidability of typing requires that certain operations of the OPM are decidable.

Definition 4.2 (Decidable OPM).

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\odot,\varepsilon,\leq)
be an OPM.
We say
\mathcal{M}
is
decidable
,
iff for all
x,y\in M
it is decidable whether

x=y
,
x\leq y
, and
\exists z\in M.\ x\odot z\leq y
.

Theorem 4.3 (Decidability).

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a decidable OPM, then

(1)

for all
\Gamma
and
M^{\uparrow}
it is decidable if
there exist
T
,
e
, and
M
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}
.

(2)

for all
\Gamma
,
M^{\downarrow}
and T it is decidable if
there exist
e
and
M
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\downarrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\downarrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}
.

Proof.

Mutual rule induction on the typing derivations.
A detailed proof can be found in our Agda formalization
in the file
LawAndOrder/Algorithmic/TypingDecidable.agda
.
∎

4.2.
Implementation

We have implemented a type-checker and interpreter for our
language in Rust using OPMs of regular expressions as resources.
In contrast to
Example

2.2
, we do not fix a
particular envelope language, but assign each resource the envelope language
according to the regular expression with which it was created.
That is, we can describe resources by arbitrary non-empty regular
languages.

Here is an example program supported by our implementation that
revisits
Example

2.2
.

⬇

1

let

copy

:

{
r
*}

\otimes

{
w
*}

-[
u

1]
\rightarrow

Unit

2

copy

(
rc
,

wc
)

=

drop

(!{
r
}

rc
);

drop

(!{
w
}

wc
)

3

in

4

let

if0

=

new

{(
r
|
w
)*
c
}

in

let

of0

=

new

{(
r
|
w
)*
c
}

in

5

let

b1
,

if1

=

split

{
r
*}

if0

in

let

b2
,

of1

=

split

{
w
*}

of0

in

6

let

_

=

copy

(
b1
,

b2
)

in

7

drop

(!{
c
}

if1
);

drop

(!{
c
}

of1
)

In the implementation, a resource type is written as
\{r\}
where
r

is a regular expression. The expression
new

{(
r
|
w
)*
c
}
(in
line 4) creates a new resource with envelope language

E=(r|w)^{*}c
. Line 5 splits off a borrow for reading (writing)
operations indicated by the
{
r
*}
(
{
w
*}
) argument
to split. The implementation computes the best continuation of a split from
the type of the borrow: Its resource corresponds to the
product
derivative
(a variation of the standard language derivative
operation
(
Suzuki and Okui, 2008
)
) of the argument
resource by the borrowed resource. In the example, the product
derivative of
E
by
r^{*}
(
w^{*}
) is again
E
. The function

copy
, defined in lines 1/2, takes an unordered pair of
borrows for reading and writing and returns the unit value. The
function type is annotated with
u
for unrestricted and

1
for the latent effect of the function (it performs
resource operations). The function body performs a read and a write
operation and discards the borrows using the
drop

operation. The expression
!{
r
}

rc

symbolizes a resource operation for
{
r
}
, i.e., a single
read. The type would allow further reads and writes. In line 9, we
close the resources using
!{
c
}
and discard them
afterwards.

The implementation mostly saves the programmer from making ordered
types explicit. It infers a viable ordering mode for a
pair, a function, or a let by working through the rules from least
restrictive to most restrictive: first the unrestricted variant, then
the unordered linear variant, and finally the ordered variants. We
illustrate this approach with the let expressions in the
example.

Line 1

This let binds an unrestricted value, so it creates an
unrestricted binding.

Line 4.1

adds an ordered binding to the context.

Line 4.2

the second let in this line is more tricky. If we
interpreted this let as ordered, we would create an artificial
constraint between
if0
and
of0
. But using
the the unordered linear variant is successful and results in
independent bindings for
if0
and
of0
.

Line 5

each split creates two ordered pair bindings, but the
pairs are independent, so the second let must be unordered linear,
again. Here is a place where context-contexts come into play: we
have to replace the ordered binding
of0
by

b2
,
of1
without disturbing the surrounding context
structure.

Line 6

creates an unrestricted binding. The variables

b1
and
b2
are correctly removed.

Line 7

processes the remaining bindings. This line has similar
issues as a let, because the semicolon can also be interpreted as
independent or sequential.

5.
Related Work

5.1.
Aliasing, Linear Types, and Ordered Types

The issue of aliasing has prompted
extensive research into methods for restricting and analyzing aliases

(
Clarke et al., 2013
)
. However, these approaches tend to be
quite intricate. For instance, Boyland’s fractional permissions

(
Boyland, 2003
)

treat permissions for memory cells algebraically, allowing permissions
to be divided among multiple references. By doing so, if the
references are combined, the original (complete) permission can be
recovered. However, aside from basic reference counting, general
fractional permissions have not seen widespread adoption in practical
languages.

A lot of investigation has focused on ownership types

(
Clarke et al., 1998
)
, of which there are many
variations. Ownership types enforce encapsulation by ensuring
that an object’s reference remains within its owner’s control. Our
work focuses on ensuring sound typestate semantics.

Gordon et al.
(
Gordon et al., 2012
)
outline a type
system employing permissions to facilitate safe concurrency. While
this work concentrates on concurrency, it does not assist in reasoning
about object protocols, as typestate does.

Substructural logic imposes restrictions on the principles of
weakening, contraction, and exchange of hypotheses that are common in
intuitionistic as well as classical logic. Most famously, linear logic
elides weakening and contraction, so that hypotheses must be used
exactly once. This property makes linear logic a powerful foundation
for reasoning about resources
(
Girard, 1987
)
.

Wadler (1990)
brought
linear types in focus of much research,
yet they were not widely adopted. Rust

(
Matsakis and Klock II, 2014
;
Team, 2024
)
stands as an exception, utilizing a form
of linearity to confine aliases to mutable objects. However, this
limited usage of linearity in Rust does not necessitate a
sophisticated permission system; for instance, Rust types cannot
directly express the states of referenced objects. Alms

(
Tov and Pucella, 2011
)
is an ML-like language supporting affine
types, but not linearity. Typestate is not directly supported, but it
could be encoded. Session types

(
Hüttel et al., 2016
)
offer another approach to
linear types in programming languages, as seen in Concurrent C0

(
Willsey et al., 2016
)
. However, session types are more
directly suited to communicating concurrent processes. Recent versions
of Haskell support linearity

(
Bernardy et al., 2018
;
Spiwack et al., 2022
)
, so that typestate could also be
encoded with some limitations.

Ahmed et al. (2007)
defined a core functional programming language

\lambda
URAL
supporting strong updates, i.e., changing the type of
an object in a reference cell. They employ substructural typing in
several flavors:
U
nrestricted,
R
elevant,

A
ffine, and Linear. They introduced two
languages, L3 and extended L3. While L3 permits aliasing, it only
allows exclusive access through a capability, restricting a single
reference’s ability to read/write to an object. In contrast, full,
shared, and pure access permissions allow for a broader range of
aliasing patterns. Extended L3 enables the recovery of a capability,
but programmers must prove that no other capabilities to the reference
cell exist. Extended L3 operates within a parametrized framework,
requiring the addition of one’s own type system to associate proof
with the capability request.

Marshall and Orchard (2023)
apply the theory of graded
types to control ownership and uniqueness. In their calculus,
permissions are described by a grade algebra, i.e., a preordered
semiring. We were expecting such structures to model resources, but
found them inadequate in our examples.

Ordered logic goes one step beyond linear logic in eliding exchange.
The Lambek calculus
(
Lambek, 1958
;
Lambek, 1960
)
uses this rigid structure to reason
about sentence structure in natural languages. There are further
foundational studies exploring non-commutative logic

(
Abrusci, 1990
;
Abrusci and Ruet, 1999
)
, but they are not
geared towards computer science applications.

Polakow and Pfenning (1999a)
investigate the proof theory of
intuitionistic non-commutative linear logic (INCLL). Their presentation is based on
natural deduction and comes with a term calculus. They prove subject
reduction and canonicity and thus establish their calculus as a
foundation for a logical framework.
The typing rules of their calculus differ subtly from ours: they are
defined from a linear logic point of view, where every assumption is
linear, and their hypotheses are categorized in intuitionistic,
linear, or ordered contexts. In contrast, types in our system carry substructural
qualifications that determine their possible modes of use, thus our
contexts hold a mixture of intuitionistic, linear, and ordered
hypotheses.

Polakow and Pfenning (1999b)
extends the study of INCLL by
considering a sequent calculus and developing some of its proof
theory.

Polakow (2000)
further extends this development with
proof search in the style of logic programming.
Polakow’s thesis
(
Polakow, 2001
)
offers a
comprehensive review of the developments arising from INCLL.

Polakow and Yi (2001)
present an application of an
ordered logical framework to obtain a theoretical foundation for a
stack-based compilation technique for exceptions.
DeYoung’s thesis
(
DeYoung, 2020
)
provides a more
recent overview of further work on INCLL and related systems.

Walker (2005)
discusses substructural type systems in
general. One part introduces an ordered type system with the goal to
“provide a foundation for managing memory allocated on the
stack”. The idea is to have the ordering on type assumptions
guarantee that stack-allocated values are used in a last-in/first-out
manner. The system restricts ordering to pairs and base types, thus
avoiding the need to introduce a complex environment structure or to
discuss several distinct function spaces.

We conclude this discussion with an observation.
Most published work on ordered logic and typing relies on the core idea
that resources must not be shuffled. However, it does not
preclude reasoning steps that process resources “in the middle” of
a sequence of resources.
This view is quite different from our use of order. We require that
resources are processed in the prescribed sequence and do not permit
operations on resources in the middle of a sequence.

5.2.
Typestate

Strom and Yemini
(
Strom and Yemini, 1986
)
introduced the
concept of typestate for programming languages. The main difficulty in
making typestate useful is tracking aliases. Doing so is easy in the
presence of linearly handled resources, but real programs are rarely
written in this way so that any typestate system has to deal with
aliasing, e.g., by supporting a concept of borrowing.
Often, a typestate checker is implemented as an additional analysis on
top of a (typed) program. Techniques rooted in abstract
interpretation, such as those discussed by Fink et al.
(
Fink et al., 2008
)
, perform
a global alias analysis and typically assume the correct implementation of the
protocol in the resource class while focusing on verifying client
conformance. Naeem and Lhoták
(
Naeem and Lhoták, 2008
)
devised an analysis for
scrutinizing typestate properties across multiple interacting
objects. Such global analyses are expensive as they execute on the
entire code base of the system.

Fugue
(
DeLine and Fähndrich, 2004
)
was the first modular typestate
verification system for object-oriented programs. It categorizes
objects as "unaliased" or "maybe aliased", and permits state changes
only for "unaliased" objects.
Bierhoff and Aldrich (2007)
extended
this approach by introducing expressive run-time dependent method specifications based on
linear logic
(
Girard, 1987
)
. They introduced access permissions to
enable state changes in the presence of aliasing. They rely on
fractional permissions
(
Boyland, 2003
)
to
accommodate patterns like borrowing and adoption

(
Boyland and Retert, 2005
)
. Plural, a tool for modular
typestate checking with
access permissions for Java, has been studied in several practical
examples
(
Bierhoff et al., 2009
)
. Nanda et al.
(
Nanda et al., 2005
)
outlined a
system for deriving typestate information from Java
programs.

Distributed session types
(
Gay et al., 2015
)
offer a similar
expressiveness to Plural, albeit with protocols based on
structural types rather than nominal typestates. They deal
with communication over distributed channels and object protocols but
do not permit aliasing for objects with protocols.

Voinea et al
(
Voinea et al., 2020
)
have developed a static
checker for typestate protocols in Java based on ideas from session types.
Bacchiani et al
(
Bacchiani et al., 2022
)
presented a
tool that statically verifies that during the execution of a Java
program: sequences of method calls obey the objects’ protocols; the
objects’ protocols are completed; there are no null-pointer
exceptions; instances of subclasses respect the
protocol of their superclasses.

The aforementioned approaches do not integrate typestate within the
programming model, but rather overlay a static typestate analysis on
top of an existing language. Typestate-oriented programming (TSOP),
proposed by Aldrich et al.
(
Aldrich et al., 2009
)
, distinguishes itself by supporting
changes to object representations at run time both in the statics as
in the dynamics. The programming language Plaid was the first to
integrate typestates in its programming model. Saini et al.
(
Saini et al., 2010
)

developed a core calculus for a TSOP language, which is object-based
and relies on structural types. Featherweight Typestate

(
Garcia et al., 2014
)
builds upon this work but
adapts it to a class-based, nominal approach with shared access
permissions and state guarantees for reasoning about typestate in the
presence of aliasing. Subsequently it has been extended to a
concurrent setting
(
Gerbo and Padovani, 2019
)
. Earlier work related to TSOP includes the Fickle
system
(
Drossopoulou et al., 2001
)
, which is capable of changing an object’s
class at run time (dynamic reclassification) but with limited ability
to reason about field states.

Igarashi and Kobayashi (2002)
;
Kobayashi et al. (2006)
;
Iwama et al. (2006)

propose a type system for
resource usage
, which analyzes how a program accesses resources.
Their uses are expressed in terms of sets of sequences of program
points, denoted by a usage algebra with sophisticated operators.
Their system can analyze arbitrary
programs including interleaving usage patterns of aliases, whereas our
system is prescriptive as it sequentializes the processing of aliases.

We conclude this discussion with two observations.

Several typestate systems can reason about programs with multiple
active aliases where operations can be interleaved.
State
guarantees

(
Garcia et al., 2014
)
are an example
for such a mechanism.
We can only implement a sequential state guarantee by using an index
structure with sufficiently high granularity. In fact, the first
example for split in the introduction is such an example.

All existing typestate systems are state-oriented, in the sense that resources are annotated with pairs
of input and output state (cf.
Section

2.4
), whereas our system is
transition-oriented as we ask for (an abstraction of)
a sequence of operations. We believe that the latter provides significant flexibility as it can
encompass all transitions that result from applying the operations; a
state-oriented system would have to resort to structures like
intersection types or the logical specifications of
Bierhoff and Aldrich (2007)
.
That is, our view to state change is dual to the traditional view.

6.
Conclusion

We presented a novel transition-based foundation for typestate that
admits borrowing and has a range of interesting applications. Our
paper contributes a new perspective on ordered types using inspiration
from BI.
We believe that the shift from a state-based
presentation of typestate to a transition-based one can address a concern
that is often raised in the context of typestate. As

Coblenz et al. (2020, Sec. 4)
put it: “[A language]
including typestate could result in a design that was hard to
use, since typical typestate languages require users to understand a
complex permission model”. In our design, users have to
understand the resource API and traces. Future user studies will have
to clarify the usability of the transition-based approach.

Data Availability Statement

A full version of this paper with all proofs is available as a
technical report
(
Saffrich et al., 2024
)
.
The implementation is available in a GitHub repository

https://github.com/m0rphism/bsession-impl.git
.

Appendix A
Algorithmic Typing

Definition A.1 (Context Restriction).

Let
\Gamma
be a context and
X
be a set of variables.
The
context restriction
of
\Gamma
with respect to
X
is defined as

\displaystyle{\cdot}\mathop{\downarrow_{\cdot}}

\displaystyle:\mathtt{Context}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathtt{Var})\to\mathtt{Context}

\displaystyle{\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\cdot&\Gamma=\cdot\\
x:T&\Gamma=x:T\land x\in X\\
\cdot&\Gamma=x:T\land x\not\in X\\
({\Gamma_{1}}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}})\mathop{,}({\Gamma_{2}}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}})&\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\mathop{,}\Gamma_{2}\\
({\Gamma_{1}}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}})\mathop{\|}({\Gamma_{2}}\mathop{\downarrow_{X}})&\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}\end{cases}

\Gamma
be a context and

2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{(S\twoheadrightarrow_{e}T)}\mid{0}\Rightarrow{M}\quad{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{N^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{S}\mid{e_{2}}\Rightarrow{N}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{(\mathop{M^{\uparrow}}{N^{\uparrow}})}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{(e\mathop{\sqcup}e_{2})}\Rightarrow{(M{}^{<}N)}}\quad\textsc{AT-LApp}

Algorithmic Typing (Inference Rules, Part I)

\frac{\Gamma_{1}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({M^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma_{2}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({N^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}\quad\quad{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip T)}\mid{(e_{1}\mathop{\sqcup}e_{2})}\Rightarrow{(M\otimes N)}}\quad\textsc{AT-UPair}\frac{\Gamma_{1}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({M^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma_{2}={\Gamma}\mathop{\downarrow_{\texttt{fv}({N^{\uparrow}})}}\Gamma\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{,}\Gamma_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma\not\lesssim\Gamma_{1}\mathop{\|}\Gamma_{2}\mathop{\texttt{ord}}{S}\texttt{ implies }e_{2}=0\quad\quad{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash{M^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{S}\mid{e_{1}}\Rightarrow{M}{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash{N^{\uparrow}}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{({M^{\downarrow}}:{T})}{\hskip 2.84526pt\uparrow\hskip 2.84526pt}{T}\mid{e}\Rightarrow{M}}\quad\textsc{AT-Ann}

.
Algorithmic Typing (Inference Rules, Part II)

Figure
11
,

12
, and

13

shows the rules for the algorithmic typing relation.

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
3.13

Proposition B.1 (Proposition 3.13 in the paper).

Contexts
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
are related by applying the monoid laws,
commutativity of unordered composition, and demotion with
unrestricted bindings, if and only if
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

We start by formally defining a syntactic relation mentioned in the property as follows.

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\cdot,\Gamma\equiv\Gamma

[CIdL]

\Gamma,\cdot\equiv\Gamma

[CIdR]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\equiv(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}),\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}}

[CAssoc]

\cdot\parallel\Gamma\equiv\Gamma

[PId]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}

[PComm]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}})\equiv(\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}}

[PAssoc]

\mathtt{unr}\,T

\mathcal{G}[x\mathord{:}T]\equiv\mathcal{G}[\cdot]\parallel x\mathord{:}T

[UUnr]

\mathtt{unr}\,T

x\mathord{:}T\parallel x\mathord{:}T\equiv x\mathord{:}T

[UDemo]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\mathcal{G}[\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\equiv\mathcal{G}[\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]

[Ctx]

\Gamma\equiv\Gamma

[Refl]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

[Sym]

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}}

\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{3}}}

[Trans]

The first three rules determine the monoid lows for the ordered composition.
The next three rules determine the monoid lows and commutativity for the unordered composition.

(UUnr)
and
(UDemo)
are about unrestricted bindings.

(UUnr)
characterizes embed treatment of unrestricted bindings; that is their position in a context is irrelevant.

(UDemo)
determines the demotion (contraction) of unrestricted bindings.
The last four rules give the congruence relation as usual.

Now we can re-state the proposition more formally as follows.

Proposition B.2.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}};S_{1}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}};S_{2}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
Then,
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
iff
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
S_{1}=S_{2}
.

It is rather straightforward to show the only-if direction (left to right) by induction on the given derivation (in the cases
(UUnr)
and
(Ctx)
require other induction proofs though).
So, we concentrate on a proof of the if direction (right to left).

The first observation to show the property is that we can split unrestricted bindings out from a typing context by using
(UUnr)
.
That is, for any
\Gamma
, there is a syntactic equivalent
\Gamma^{\prime}
, namely
\Gamma\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}
, of the form
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}\parallel x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\dots\parallel x_{k}\mathord{:}T_{k}
where
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{0}}}
has no unrestricted bindings and all
T_{{\mathrm{1}}}\dots T_{k}
are unrestricted.
Using this observation, we can examine the restricted bindings and unrestricted bindings separately and see that the unrestricted part (
S_{1}=S_{2}
) of semantic equivalence can be related syntactically via that form.

So, in the following, we discuss how to relate the restricted binding part (
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
) of semantic equivalence syntactically.
Thus, from here, we only consider typing contexts not containing unrestricted bindings, that is
\Gamma
such that
[\![\Gamma]\!]=\mathfrak{G};\emptyset
; and, for ease of writing, we abuse
[\![\Gamma]\!]
to denote the graph part
\mathfrak{G}
.

The proof strategy is as follows.
Firstly, we define
realization
of a graph representation
\mathfrak{G}
, denoted by
\lceil\mathfrak{G}\rceil
, which is a partial function from graph representations to typing contexts providing a syntactic presentation of
\mathfrak{G}
.
Note that the realization is surely partial as some graph representations have no corresponding typing context.
For instance, no typing context is interpreted as the following graph.
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}
x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}
x_{{\mathrm{3}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{3}}}
x_{{\mathrm{4}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{4}}}

Even so, we will show the following proposition that says given realization suffices for interpretations of typing contexts, meaning that it deserves our purpose.

Proposition B.3.

\lceil[\![\Gamma]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma
.

Lastly, we show the following proposition.

Proposition B.4.

If
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\simeq[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
, then
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil
.

Combining the propositions, we can see
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
if
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\simeq[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
as
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

We start by defining several
cuts
.
Since the vertices of a graph representation are indexed by natural numbers, we can specify a cut by a natural number.
We formally define the sense by giving the pair of induced sub-graphs from a given natural number as follows.

Definition B.5 (Index cut).

Given
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
and
n^{\prime}
such that
0<n^{\prime}<n
, we define a pair of graph representations, denoted by
(\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n^{\prime}},\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}})
, as follows.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(n^{\prime},v|_{\mathbb{N}_{<n^{\prime}}},\{(i,j)\in E\mid i,j<n^{\prime}\})

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(n-n^{\prime},i\mapsto v(i+n^{\prime}),\{(i-n^{\prime},j-n^{\prime})\mid(i,j)\in E\wedge n^{\prime}\leq i,j\})

Note that interpretations of typing contexts are closed under index cut.

Lemma B.6.

For any index cut
([\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}},[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}})
, there exist
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma^{\prime}]\!]
.

Proof.

By structural induction on
\Gamma
.

•

(
\Gamma=\cdot
or
\Gamma=b
)
Let
[\![\Gamma]\!]=(n,v,E)
.
In these cases,
n\leq 1
.
Thus, we have no index cut for
[\![\Gamma]\!]
.
Consequently, this case is vacuously true.

•

(
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}})

In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
Let
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
.
We consider the order relationship between
n^{\prime}
and
n_{1}
.

–

(
n^{\prime}<n_{1}
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}
.
So, we have some
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
such that
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
.
We choose
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
as
\Gamma^{\prime}
for the goal.

–

(
n^{\prime}=n_{1}
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
.
So, choosing
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
as
\Gamma^{\prime}
suffices the goal.

–

(
n^{\prime}>n_{1}
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]_{<n^{\prime}-n_{1}}
.
We have
\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
such that
[\![\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]_{<n^{\prime}-n_{1}}
by IH.
We choose
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
as
\Gamma^{\prime}
to show the goal.
In fact,
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}}
.

•

(
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
) Similar to the case above.
∎

Lemma B.7.

For any index cut
([\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}},[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}})
, there exist
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
[\![\Gamma]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}=[\![\Gamma^{\prime}]\!]
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof or
lemma

B.6
. ∎

We define the following characteristic cuts to define the realization.

Definition B.8 (Union cut).

Given
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
, we call a natural number
n^{\prime}

union cut
iff

•

0<n^{\prime}<n
; and

•

(i,j)\notin E
for any
0\leq i<n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}\leq j<n
.

Definition B.9 (Join cut).

Given
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
, we call a natural number
n^{\prime}

join cut
iff

•

0<n^{\prime}<n
; and

•

(i,j)\in E
for any
0\leq i<n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}\leq j<n
.

Union and join cut correspond to the connecting point of the union and join of graph representations, respectively, as we state as follows.

Lemma B.10.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
where
n_{1},n_{2}>0
.
Then,
n_{1}
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and
(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}){}_{<n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}){}_{\geq n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

Lemma B.11.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
where
n_{1},n_{2}>0
.
Then
n_{1}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and
(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}){}_{<n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}){}_{\geq n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

We put the fact union cuts of a graph representation do not influence each other.
A similar property holds for join cuts.
Such properties are handy for doing mathematical induction proof for the number of union and join cuts.
Note that, however, a union cut and a join cut will influence each other; that is a join cut may spawn in a sub-graph, obtained by a union cut.

Lemma B.12.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}=(n^{\prime},v^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and
n
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}
.
Then,

•

n
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}\cup\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
; and

•

n+n^{\prime}
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\cup\mathfrak{G}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

Lemma B.13.

Suppose
n_{1}<n_{2}
are union cuts of
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
.
Then,

•

n_{1}
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
; and

•

n_{2}-n_{1}
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

Lemma B.14.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}=(n^{\prime},v^{\prime},E^{\prime})
and
n
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}
.
Then,

•

n
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}*\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
; and

•

n+n^{\prime}
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}*\mathfrak{G}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

Lemma B.15.

Suppose
n_{1}<n_{2}
are join cuts of
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
.
Then,

•

n_{1}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n_{{\mathrm{2}}}}
; and

•

n_{2}-n_{1}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n_{{\mathrm{1}}}}
.

Proof.

By definition. ∎

Now we define the realization accompanied by the following lemma that guarantees the well-definedness.

Lemma B.16.

For any
\mathfrak{G}
, either union cuts or join cuts can exclusively exist.

Proof.

By contradiction.
Suppose there exist both union and join cuts for some
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
.
Then, we have
(0,n-1)\notin E
and
(0,n-1)\in E
by the condition of the union and join cuts, respectively; but they contradict each other.
∎

Definition B.17 (Realization).

For
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
, we define
\lceil\mathfrak{G}\rceil
inductively for
n
as follows.

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{G}\rceil=\begin{cases}\cdot&\text{if $n=0$}\\
v(0)&\text{if $n=1$}\\
\lceil\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n^{\prime}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\rceil&\text{if there exist the minimum union cut $n^{\prime}$ for $\mathfrak{G}$}\\
\lceil\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n^{\prime}}\rceil,\lceil\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\rceil&\text{if there exist the minimum join cut $n^{\prime}$ for $\mathfrak{G}$}\end{cases}

As we mentioned, realization is a partial function.
Moreover, it does not work correctly in general.
A simple troubling case is shown in the following diagram, where the interpretation of the realization of the graph representation is not isomorphic to the original graph representation.

One might think that is caused by the definitions of union and join cuts, where we ignore inverse direction edges, that is ones from larger indexed vertices to smaller indexed ones.
However, it is not.
We can find another troubling case even if we fix the definitions taking inverse direction edges into account.

Anyway, the realization works correctly for the range of
[\![-]\!]
, as we will show
proposition

B.3
.
The key insight to show the proposition is the interpretation of a typing context is always topologically ordered, no inverse direction edges exist.

Definition B.18 (Topologically ordered).

We say
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)

topologically ordered
iff the indentify function for
\mathbb{N}_{<n}
is a topological ordering of
\mathfrak{G}
.
In other words,
n_{1}<n_{2}
for any
(n_{1},n_{2})\in E
.

Lemma B.19.

[\![\Gamma]\!]
is topologically ordered.

Proof.

By structural induction on
\Gamma
. ∎

For a topologically ordered graph representation, we can recover the original graph from the two sub-graphs obtained by a union or join cut of the graph by the union or join of them, respectively.

Lemma B.20.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}
is topologically ordered.
If
n
is a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}
, then
\mathfrak{G}=\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n}\cup\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n}
.

Proof.

By definition.
∎

Lemma B.21.

Suppose
\mathfrak{G}
is topologically ordered.
If
n
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}
, then
\mathfrak{G}=\mathfrak{G}{}_{<n}*\mathfrak{G}{}_{\geq n}
.

Proof.

By definition.
∎

Using the lemmas, we can relate the semantic composition (union and join) and syntactic composition (parallel and concatenate) under the realization as follows.

Lemma B.22.

For any topologically ordered
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
,
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
is defined iff both
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil
and
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
are defined, and
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil\equiv\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
.

Proof.

If either
n_{1}
or
n_{2}
is 0, we can have the goal by
(PId)
and
(PComm)
.
So, we show the case
n_{1},n_{2}>0
by mathematical induction on the number of union cuts of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

•

(Base case)
In this case,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
has no union cut.
Then,
n_{1}
is the minimum union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
That is because if there exists a smaller union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
than
n_{1}
, it must be a union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, too; that contradicts this case’s assumption.
So, we have
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil=\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
by definition and
lemma

B.10
.
As a result the goal can be derived by
(Refl)
.

•

(Induction step)
In this case,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
has some union cut.
So, let
n^{\prime}
be the minimum union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil=\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\rceil

We can see
n^{\prime}
is the minimum union cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, too.
So, we have

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil=\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil

Here, we can see
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil\equiv\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\rceil\parallel\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
by IH.
Therefore, we can have the goal by
(PAssoc)
. ∎

Lemma B.23.

For any topologically ordered
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
,
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
is defined iff both
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil
and
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
are defined, and
\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil\equiv\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\rceil,\lceil\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\rceil
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
lemma

B.22
, using
lemma

B.11
and
lemma

B.21
. ∎

Now we can prove
proposition

B.3
.

Lemma B.24 (
proposition

B.3
).

\lceil[\![\Gamma]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma
.

Proof.

We prove the proposition by structural induction on
\Gamma
.

•

(Cases
\Gamma=\cdot
and
\Gamma=b
)
In these cases,
\lceil[\![\Gamma]\!]\rceil=\Gamma
by definition.
So, we can have the goal
\lceil[\![\Gamma]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma
by
(Refl)
.

•

(Case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
We have

(IH1)

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}

(IH2)

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

by IH.
Now we can have the goal as follows

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma]\!]\rceil

\displaystyle=\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil

\displaystyle\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\parallel\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil

by
lemmas

B.19
and
B.22

\displaystyle\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

by (IH1), (IH2), and
(Ctx)

\displaystyle=\Gamma

•

(Case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
)
Similar to the case above, using
lemma

B.23
.
∎

To show
proposition

B.4
, we need another observation for the range of
[\![-]\!]
.
That is, any disconnected interpretation is always constructed by the union of other interpretations.
It is not sufficient that the interpretation is topologically ordered, as the following graph is topologically ordered and disconnected but has no union cut.
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{1}}}
x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{2}}}
x_{{\mathrm{3}}}\mathord{:}T_{{\mathrm{3}}}

We formalize the observation as follows.

Definition B.25 (Weakly connected).

Given
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
, we call two vertices in
\mathfrak{G}
indexed by
n_{x}
and
n_{y}

weakly connected
iff there exists a sequence
\{n_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq m}
such that
n_{x}=n_{0}
,
n_{y}=n_{m}
, and
(n_{i},n_{i+1})\in E
or
(n_{i+1},n_{i})\in E
for any
0\leq i<m
.
We call a graph representation itself weakly connected iff any two vertices in the graph representation are weakly connected.
We call a graph representation
weakly disconnected
iff it is not weakly connected.

Definition B.26 (Union decomposition).

Given
\mathfrak{G}=(n,v,E)
, we call a non-empty sequence of weakly connected non-null graph representations
\{\mathfrak{G}_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq m}

union decomposition
of
\mathfrak{G}
iff
\mathfrak{G}=\mathfrak{G}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}_{m}
.

Lemma B.27.

If
[\![\Gamma]\!]
is non-null, then there exits a sequence
\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq m}
such that
\{[\![\Gamma_{i}]\!]\}_{0\leq i\leq m}
is a union decomposition of
[\![\Gamma]\!]
.

Proof.

By structural induction on
\Gamma
.

•

(
\Gamma=\cdot
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]
is null.
So, the property is vacuously true.

•

(
\Gamma=b
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]
is weakly connected.
So, by choosing the singleton sequence
\{\Gamma\}
, we can show the goal.
Note that
[\![\Gamma]\!]
is non-null by the assumption.

•

(
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\parallel\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
)
In this case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\cup[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
If either
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
or
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is null,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
or
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
, respectively.
So, in either case, we have the goal directly by IH.
Otherwise, both
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is non-null.
In that case, we have sequences
\{\Gamma^{1}_{i}\}_{0\leq i<m_{1}}
and
\{\Gamma^{2}_{j}\}_{0\leq j<m_{2}}
satisfying the condition of the property against
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, respectively, by IH.
Now, we can see the concatenation of them satisfies the condition of property against
\Gamma
.

•

(
\Gamma=\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}},\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
)
In this case
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]*[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
If either
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
or
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is null,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
or
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
, respecitvely.
So, in either case, we have the goal directly by IH.
Otherwise,
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is non-null.
In that case,
[\![\Gamma]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]*[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is weakly connected.
So, by choosing the singleton sequence
\{\Gamma\}
, we can show the goal.
Note that
[\![\Gamma]\!]
is non-null by the assumption.
∎

Lemma B.28.

Let
\{\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq n_{1}}
and
\{\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq n_{2}}
be union decompositions.
If
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{n_{1}}\sim\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{n_{2}}
, we have a one-to-one isomorphic correspondence between the elements of them; that is

•

n_{1}=n_{2}
; and

•

there exists a bijection
g
between
\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}
such that
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{i}\sim\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{g(i)}
for any
0\leq i\leq n_{1}
.

Proof.

For convenience, let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{n_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{n_{2}}
.
We show the property by mathematical induction on
n_{1}
.

•

(Base case
n_{1}=0
)
We consider the following two cases.

–

(
n_{2}=0
) In this sub-case, the first sub-goal
n_{1}=n_{2}
is obvious.
Regarding the second sub-goal, we know
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{0}
.
So, it suffices to choose
g(x)=x
, where
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}\sim\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{0}
by the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

–

(
n_{2}>0
)
In this sub-case, we can pick distinct components
\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{y}
, that is
x\neq y
, from
\{\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq n_{2}}
.
Since they are non-null, there exist vertices
a
and
b
in
\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{x}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{y}
, respectively.
We can see
a
and
b
are weakly disconnected in
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, but the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
maps
a
and
b
into
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}
, which is weakly connected, that is a contradiction.
Therefore, this sub-case cannot happen.

•

(Induction step
n_{1}>0
)
Let
f
be the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
We can see
f
maps all vertices in
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}
into all vertices in some
\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{k}
and this mapping becomes the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{G}j^{2}_{k}
.
Furthermore, we can see
\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{n_{1}}\sim\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{0}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{k-1}\cup\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{k+1}\cup\dots\cup\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{n_{2}}
.
So, we have
n_{1}-1=n_{2}-1
and one-to-one correspondence
g^{\prime}
by IH.
We choose
g
as follows.

\displaystyle g(i)=\begin{cases}k&\text{if $i=0$}\\
g^{\prime}(i-1)&\text{if $g^{\prime}(i-1)<k$}\\
g^{\prime}(i-1)+1&\text{if $g^{\prime}(i-1)>k$}\end{cases}

∎

We need to consider decomposition by join cuts, too.
However, it is rather well-organized than union cuts, that is no commute happens among components, and so, what we need is the following lemma.

Lemma B.29.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n,v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n,v_{2},E_{2})
be topologically ordered graph representations such that
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
Then,
n^{\prime}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
iff
c
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
Furthermore,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Since
\simeq
is symmetric, it suffices to show only one direction.
Let
f
be the isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, meaning that

(H1)

f
is bijective

(H2)

\displaystyle v_{1}\circ f=v_{2}

(H3)

\displaystyle E_{1}=\{(f(x),f(y))\mid(x,y)\in E_{2}\}

Supposing
n^{\prime}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, we have

(H4)

\displaystyle 0<n^{\prime}<n

(H5)

\displaystyle(x,y)\in E_{2}\text{ for any }x<n^{\prime}\text{ and }n^{\prime}\leq y

by definition.
Now we will show
f(x)<n^{\prime}
for any
x<n^{\prime}
by contradiction, that implies
n^{\prime}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
by (H4) and (H5).
If
f(x)\geq n^{\prime}
, there exists
y
such that
n^{\prime}\leq y
and
f(y)<n^{\prime}
since
f
is bijective.
However, this contradicts the fact that
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is topologically ordered, as
(f(x),f(y))\in E_{1}
by (H3) and (H5), while
f(y)<f(x)
.
Therefore,
n^{\prime}
is a join cut of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, too.
As a side-result, we can see
f|_{\mathbb{N}_{<n^{\prime}}}
becomse a isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}{}_{<n^{\prime}}
.
Similarly, we can have a isomorphism between
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}{}_{\geq n^{\prime}}
from
f
. ∎

Lemma B.30 (
proposition

B.4
).

If
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\simeq[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
, then
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil
.

Proof.

Suppose
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
,
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
, and let
f
be the isomorphism between
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
Note that
n_{1}=n_{2}
since
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
are isomorphic.
By
lemma

B.24
,
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil
and
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil
are defined, and
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
We prove the proposition by complete induction on
n_{1}
.
We consider whether
n_{1}
is 0, 1, or more than 1.

•

(
n_{1}=0
or
n_{1}=1
)
In these cases,
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
.
So, the goal is immediate by
(Refl)
.

•

(
n_{1}>1
)
We have some sequences
\{\Gamma^{1}_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq m_{1}}
and
\{\Gamma^{2}_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq m_{2}}
satisfying the following condition by
lemma

B.27
.

Every
[\![\Gamma^{1}_{i}]\!]
is non-null

Every
[\![\Gamma^{2}_{j}]\!]
is non-null

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma^{1}_{0}]\!]\cup\dots\cup[\![\mathfrak{G}^{1}_{m_{1}}]\!]

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]=[\![\Gamma^{2}_{0}]\!]\cup\dots\cup[\![\mathfrak{G}^{2}_{m_{2}}]\!]

Furthermore, there is a one-to-one correspondence
g
between the elements of them by
lemma

B.28
as follows.

\displaystyle m_{1}=m_{2}

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma^{1}_{i}]\!]\sim[\![\Gamma^{2}_{g(i)}]\!]\text{ for any $0\leq i\leq m_{1}$}

We also have the following relation by using
lemma

B.22
(rigorously we need mathematical induction), where we suppose
\parallel
is right-associative, not important though.

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma^{1}_{0}]\!]\rceil\parallel\dots\parallel\lceil[\![\Gamma^{1}_{m_{1}}]\!]\rceil

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma^{2}_{0}]\!]\rceil\parallel\dots\parallel\lceil[\![\Gamma^{2}_{m_{2}}]\!]\rceil

Now we consider the following two sub-cases.

–

(
m_{1}=0
)
In this sub-case,
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
is weakly connected.
So, there exists the minimum join cut
n^{\prime}_{1}
of
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
for which

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil=\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rceil,\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rceil.

Similarly, there exists the minimum join cut
n^{\prime}_{2}
of
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
for which

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil=\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rceil,\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rceil.

Here, we can see

\displaystyle n^{\prime}_{1}=n^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\simeq[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

\displaystyle[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\simeq[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}

by
lemma

B.29
.
That because
n^{\prime}_{1}
and
n^{\prime}_{2}
is a join cut of
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]
and
[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]
, respectively, too, by the lemma, so
n^{\prime}_{1}\neq n^{\prime}_{2}
contradicts minimality of them.
Since we know interpretation is closed under index cuts by
lemmas

B.6
and
B.7
, now we have

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{<n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rceil

\displaystyle\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}}}\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]{}_{\geq n^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{2}}}}\rceil

by IH.
Using them, we can have the goal
\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}]\!]\rceil
by
(Ctx)
.

–

(
m_{1}>0
)
In this sub-case, we can see that the order of
[\![\Gamma^{1}_{i}]\!]
is less than
n_{1}
for any
0\leq i\leq m_{1}
.
So, we have
\lceil[\![\Gamma^{1}_{i}]\!]\rceil\equiv\lceil[\![\Gamma^{2}_{g(i)}]\!]\rceil
for any
0\leq i<m_{1}
by IH.
So, we can have the goal by
(PAssoc)
,
(PComm)
, and
(Ctx)
. ∎

Appendix C
Detailed Proofs for Lemmas in Section
3

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(subst/void).

If
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(M)
, then
M[V/x]=M
.

C.1.
Properties for graph

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/iso).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{0}}}=(0,\emptyset,\emptyset)
.

(1)

\mathfrak{G}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{0}}}=\mathfrak{G}
.

(2)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{0}}}*\mathfrak{G}=\mathfrak{G}
.

(3)

\mathfrak{G}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{0}}}=\mathfrak{G}
.

(4)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{0}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}=\mathfrak{G}
.

(5)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

(6)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}})=(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}}
.

(7)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}})=(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}}
.

Proof.

By definition except the fifth one.
Regarding the fifth one, let
(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
We can choose
f:\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}+n_{2}}
such that
f(i)=i+n_{2}
for
0\leq i<n_{1}
and
f(i)=i-n_{1}
for
n_{1}\leq i<n_{1}+n_{2}
as an witness bijection.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/span).

(1)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

(2)

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}})\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}}
.

(3)

(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}}\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{3}}})
.

Proof.

By definition.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/iso/num/bindings).

If
(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})\sim(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
, then
|\{\,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}\mid v_{1}(n^{\prime})=b\,\}|=|\{\,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{<n_{2}}\mid v_{2}(n^{\prime})=b\,\}|
for any
b
.

Proof.

By definition,
n_{1}=n_{2}
, and we have some bijection
f
between
\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}
and
\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}
such that

(1)

\displaystyle v_{2}=v_{1}\circ f.

So, the right-hand side of the goal becomes to

\displaystyle|\{\,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}\mid v_{1}(f(n^{\prime}))=b\,\}|,

which equals the left-hand side of the goal since
f
is a bijection.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/span/num/bindings).

If
(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})\mathrel{<_{\rightarrow}}(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
, then
|\{\,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{<n_{1}}\mid v_{1}(n^{\prime})=b\,\}|=|\{\,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{<n_{2}}\mid v_{2}(n^{\prime})=b\,\}|
for any
b
.

Proof.

By definition,
n_{1}=n_{2}
and
v_{1}=v_{2}
. So, the goal is obvious.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/iso/refl).

\mathfrak{G}\simeq\mathfrak{G}
.

Proof.

We can choose the identity function as the witness bijection for the isomorphism.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/iso/join).

If
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{11}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{12}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{21}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{22}}}
, then
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{11}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{21}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{12}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{22}}}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
be the first and second isomorphism, respectively; and
n_{1}=|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{11}}}|_{\bullet}
and
n_{2}=|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{21}}}|_{\bullet}
.
Then, the following
f
becomes the isomorphism for the goal.

\displaystyle f(n)=\begin{cases}f_{1}(n)&\text{if $0\leq n<n_{1}$}\\
f_{2}(n-n_{1})&\text{if $n_{1}\leq n<n_{1}+n_{2}$}\end{cases}

∎

.

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/vert).

We write
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}/i]
to express the replacement of vertex
i
in
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
by
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}/i]=(n,v,E)
,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
, and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
.
The replacement is defined as follows.

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=n_{1}+n_{2}-1

\displaystyle v(n)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{1}(n)&(0\leq n<i)\\
v_{2}(n-i)&(i\leq n<n_{2}+i)\\
v_{1}(n-n_{2}+1)&(n_{2}+i\leq n<n_{1}+n_{2}-1)\end{cases}

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\{\,(f(n),f(m))\mid n\neq i,m\neq i,(n,m)\in E_{1}\,\}\cup{}\\
&\{\,(f(n),m)\mid i\leq m<n_{2}+i,(n,i)\in E_{1}\,\}\cup{}\\
&\{\,(n,f(m))\mid i\leq n<n_{2}+i,(i,m)\in E_{1}\,\}\cup{}\\
&\{\,(n+i,m+i)\mid(n,m)\in E_{2}\,\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\text{where }f(n)=\begin{cases}n&(0\leq n<i)\\
n+n_{2}-1&(i<n<n_{1})\end{cases}

Note that
f
is undefined for
i
, but it makes no problem because
(i,i)\notin E
for an acyclic graph.

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace).

We write
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}/b]
to express the replacement of all vertices labeled
b
in
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
by
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n,v,E)
and
S=\{\,i\mid v(i)=b\,\}
.
We define the replacement
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}/b]
as
T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}},S)
defined as follows.

(2)

\displaystyle T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}},S)=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}&|S|=0\\
T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}/i],\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}},S\setminus\{i\})&i=\mathrm{max}(S)\end{cases}

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/vert/join).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
.
Then,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/i]=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i]+\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}&(0\leq i<n_{1})\\
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i]&(n_{1}\leq i<n_{1}+n_{2}).\end{cases}

Proof.

We can simply show the proposition by definition, we need to carefully examine intervals, though.
∎

\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/vert/union).

\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i]&(n_{1}\leq i<n_{1}+n_{2}).\end{cases}

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/join).

(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/b]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})
,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})
,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(n,v,E)
,
S_{1}=\{\,i\mid v_{1}(i)=b\,\}
,
S_{2}=\{\,i\mid v_{2}(i)=b\,\}
, and
S=\{\,i\mid v(i)=b\,\}
.
We show the goal by induction on the size of
S
.

-
Case Base case.

In this case,
|S|=0
.
Therefore, we can see
|S_{1}|=|S_{2}|=0
by the definition of
v
.
So, both sides become
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
by definition.

-
Case Inductive step.

In this case, we have some
i=\mathrm{max}(S)
.
So,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/b]=T((\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/i],\mathfrak{G},S\setminus\{i\})

by definition.
We consider the following cases by
subsection C.1
.

-
SCase
0\leq i<n_{1}
.

In this case,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/i]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i]+\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}.

So, we have

\displaystyle T((\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/i],\mathfrak{G},S\setminus\{i\})=T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i],\mathfrak{G},S_{1}\setminus\{i\})+T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}},\mathfrak{G},S_{2})

by the induction hypohtesis.
Since we have
S_{1}\subseteq S
and
i=\mathrm{max}(S)
, we can have
i=\mathrm{max}(S_{1})
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/i],\mathfrak{G},S_{1}\setminus\{i\})=T(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\mathfrak{G},S_{1})

follows by definition.

-
SCase
n_{1}\leq i<n_{1}+n_{2}
.

Similar to the case above.

∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/union).

(\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}})[\mathfrak{G}/b]=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
subsection C.1
.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/replace/iso).

If
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, then
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}[\mathfrak{G}/b]
.

Proof.

Let
(n_{1},v_{1},E_{1})=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
(n_{2},v_{2},E_{2})=\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
,
S_{1}=\{i\mid v_{1}(i)=b\}
, and
S_{2}=\{i\mid v_{2}(i)=b\}
.
Note that
n_{1}=n_{2}
and
|S_{1}|=|S_{2}|
by the assumption.
We show the goal by size induction of
S_{1}
.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/ordering/exists).

For any graph representation, a topological ordering exists.

Proof.

This is a well-known property for DAGs.
We could employ this property as the acyclic condition.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/ordering/concat).

Let
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
be topological orderings of graph representations of order
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
.
Then, the
join
of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, denoted by
f_{1}+f_{2}
, is the function defined as follows.

\displaystyle(f_{1}+f_{2})(n)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{1}(n)&(0\leq n<n_{1})\\
f_{2}(n-n_{1})+n_{1}&(n_{1}\leq n<n_{1}+n_{2})\end{cases}

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/ordering/join).

If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are topological orderings of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, respectively, then
f_{1}+f_{2}
is a topological ordering of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.

Proof.

By definition.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/ordering/join/inv).

If
f
is a topological ordering of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, then there exist topological orderings
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, respectively, satisfying
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
.

Proof.

We choose
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
as follows.

\displaystyle f_{1}(n)

\displaystyle=f(n)

\displaystyle(0\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet})

\displaystyle f_{2}(n)

\displaystyle=f(n+|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet})-|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet}

\displaystyle(0\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet})

The conditions follow by the definition.
∎

Remark
\the\mythm@cnt
(graph/ordering/union).

If
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are topological orderings of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, respectively, then both

\displaystyle f(n)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{1}(n)&(0\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet})\\
f_{2}(n-|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet})+|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet}&(|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet}\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet})\end{cases}

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(n)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{2}(n)+|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}|_{\bullet}&(0\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet})\\
f_{1}(n-|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet})&(|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet}\leq n<|\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}*\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}|_{\bullet})\end{cases}

are topological orderings of
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{1}}}\cup\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{2}}}
.
